Strategy in one line. An infrastructure owner wants to know what happens when the plan meets the floor. Open with S2 (one comingle pool instead of two warehouses, a robotics ramp behind a published 98% gate that fired at 94, two go-lives with zero customer-facing incidents) and hold S18 (dual-DC WMS migration: 0.5% tolerance published before mock one, 7% → 1.8% → 0.3%, the RF-device miss owned) for the go-live-gone-wrong / Bias for Action question. Gates, invariants, triggers and hypercare numbers are the vocabulary; vendors configured, DC Ops ran the floor. v2: seven new primaries (U1–U7) re-cut the same Sephora node facts — matrix, profile, reserve, constraint, runbook, AFS control, issues log — each with its own math, behavioral pack, angle cards, trap drills and NACF bridge.
LP focus — my guess, not confirmed: Insist on the Highest Standards · Bias for Action · Deliver Results or Ownership. Amazon does not disclose the assignment; the guesses are reasoned from the interviewer's title and the JD in "Read me first" and should be held loosely. 5-Minute Cram Sheet — Rocky GoforthNEW

Profile hooks (title-based inference — no biography asserted)

  • Senior Manager, Infrastructure. Owns the physical and systems substrate: buildings, automation, WMS layer, capacity, launches, cutovers, peak readiness. His question is what happened when the plan met the floor.
  • His vocabulary: dock, space, labor, throughput, node constraints, gates, rollback, freeze windows, hypercare exit — and physical truth vs system truth.
  • My moves: open S2; name the gate that fired; put every dollar behind its basis word; end each story on one number, then one sentence to NACF inbound (doors × hours × unload rate · UPH · cube).
  • LP guess (unconfirmed): Insist on the Highest Standards · Bias for Action · Deliver Results / Ownership — Dive Deep and Earn Trust are likely second-round.

Three sentences to open with

“At Sephora’s Utah DC I turned two warehouses in one building into one comingle pool — three planning identities over one physical location — through two go-lives and a robotics ramp behind a published 98% gate: +15% processing efficiency, signed by Sephora, and zero customer-facing incidents.”

“The number that mattered most was 94 against 98: in week two the node’s constraint moved from labor hours to pick accuracy, and a throttle DC Ops owned before the ramp bought a root-caused fix for about 421 labor hours on my model — full volume in week three.”

“I ran the program on one 836-row issues log, read Blocker and Critical first — 441 rows, 98% closed — and the rows where the dock, the floor, the power and the footprint were the problem each had a name and a date.”

Five numbers never to get wrong

+15% processing efficiency · zero customer-facing incidents — record — Sephora-signed, DC Ops measured; the dollars are modeled
98% gate · week 2 at 94% · ~30% throttle for a week · full volume week 3 — record
7.0% → 1.8% → 0.3% against the 0.5% tolerance published with Finance before mock one — record
836 rows · 714 closed (85.4%) · Blocker 164 + Critical 277 = 441 (52.8%) — record; recomputed from the log with pandas
343 tasks / 48,885 task-minutes ≈ 815 h · sync < 99% for 4 h · p95 > 2× for 1 h — record

Never say: “400 devices failed” (say hundreds of RF devices) · “20+ systems” (say about a dozen impacted systems) · “fifteen tracks” (say about thirty build tracks) · any ratio of two modeled dollars · a fifty-times multiplier anywhere (the real figure in the loop is 9.7×, 7 → 68).

Question → Story RouterNEW

How to use. Hear the question, find the LP, say the 15-second opener, land the number, then stop. Every question cites the real Amazon bank wording as AIQB: LP Q#. “Card” = the angle card under that story (data · conflict/influence/backbone · failure-learning). Story IDs: S2 Utah DC comingle pool + robotics gate · S18 WMS migration, two DCs; 0.5% tolerance · U1 Node × channel × bucket-transition matrix · U2 Profile both books before the design · U3 Merch reserve — the priced no · U4 Where the node’s constraint bound · U5 343-task runbook, one irreversible instant · U6 Negative AFS + the correction screen · U7 836-row log as the readiness instrument. Basis labels are in the story; modeled / illustrative are said as such.

Dive DeepTell me about a situation that required you to dig deep to get to the root cause.
AIQB: Dive Deep Q2		U6 · data card — a negative has three causesU7 (the log, three cuts) · U2 (the profile)“A negative AFS is not one problem. I decomposed it to three cause classes — a duplicate event, a feed that had not caught up, an override — each with an owner, and every AFS-changing act got a screen, a role and an audit row.”3 cause classes; drift 0 on consecutive days (record)

I challenged it: one go-live puts a reserve-rule defect and a floor defect in the same room the same week — so January proved the rules and May moved the floor.”2 go-lives, 1 defect class each

I wrote the gate and the dial before the robots arrived and DC Ops owned them — so when week two read 94, the stop condition belonged to the people who carry the consequence.”dial owned by DC Ops before the ramp; ~30% for one week
Success and Scale Bring Broad ResponsibilityTell me about a time when you considered everyone who was affected by your work, not just your direct customers or people within your organization.

At an illustrative thirty million orders a year that is thirty thousand customers, so I treated it as a defect with an owner and a screen.”30,000 customers/yr (illustrative) → zero incidents (record)

Spend rule. One question, one story. If an LP is asked twice, move to the named backup, not a story allocated to another interviewer. S15 is Josyula’s primary — use it only if the question demands it and keep every fact identical to his packet. Real failure chapters (Apple G1/G2/G3) are the source for every failure/learning angle; none is invented.

Read me first

The interviewer's lens (inference from title + JD — not a biography)

Inference from the title. "Infrastructure" in a fulfillment network means the physical and systems substrate the process runs on — buildings, automation and robotics, the WMS/WCS layer, capacity and launches, cutovers and peak readiness. A Senior Manager there is accountable for changes that touch the floor: go-lives that cannot drop a shipment, automation ramps that must not erode throughput, and migrations where inventory accuracy cannot wobble. He will care about gates, rollback, freeze windows, hypercare exits, and what happened when the plan met the floor.

What that means for the answers. The two Sephora distribution-center programs: S2 (two systemic warehouses in one building consolidated into one comingle pool, with a WMS upgrade and a robotics ramp on the same critical path and zero customer-facing incidents allowed) and S18 (a dual-DC legacy-WMS migration run to a 0.5% reconciliation tolerance published before the first mock, with the RF-device miss owned). Tell them as Make/Deliver process redesigns — one inventory pool instead of two, a cutover discipline instead of a date — with asset utilization (+15% processing efficiency, labor-hours, capital pooled) and reliability (zero incidents, 0.3% against 0.5%) as the results. The vendor teams configured BlueYonder and Locus; DC Ops ran the floor; I owned the model, the staging, the gates and the go/no-go.

Three guessed LP focus areas, with rationale

Labeled guess. Amazon assigns 2–3 Leadership Principles per interviewer and does not disclose them. These three are inferred from the title and the JD; if the first question lands on a different LP, the twist maps under each story carry the pivot.
Guessed LPWhy this interviewer would carry itStory to open · backup
Insist on the Highest StandardsInfrastructure owners live by gates that can stop the program: the 98% robotics gate that fired at 94 (S2) and the 0.5% tolerance published with Finance before mock one (S18) are the primary and backup for this LP in the master map.S2 (open) · S18 (backup)
Bias for ActionWhen the floor breaks the plan — hundreds of RF devices failing the conversion script in a pre-training device test — he wants to see a decision taken the same day, the cost priced and the date held.S18 (the device-test miss) · S2 (the throttle to ~30%)
Deliver Results / Ownership (one of the two)Two go-lives, peak untouched, +15% signed by DC Ops; an eight-month migration on time and ~3% under budget with a $20M pipeline unblocked. Ownership shows in the RF miss carried with my name on the cause.S2 (the comingle launch) · S18 (8 months, both DCs)

Answer strategy — business first, systems as enablers

  • Open with the process problem and its cost, not the system. First sentence names the broken process (Plan, Source, Make, Deliver or Return), who it hurt (customer, Finance, the floor) and the baseline number. The platform appears only as the enabler of the redesign, in one clause.
  • Use the JD's four outcome words as the results frame: reliability (did the promise hold?), responsiveness (how much earlier did we see it?), cost efficiency ($ with its basis label), asset utilization (capital, labor-hours, capacity released).
  • Action = operating-model redesign in gated phases — Discovery → Plan & Design → Build & Test → Cutover → Hypercare, each spoken as entry bar · what I delivered · exit bar. Say the gate that fired, not the meeting that happened.
  • Every dollar carries its basis word in the same breath — record · user anchor · modeled · priced. A record dollar is said flat; a modeled dollar is said with its inputs. Never promote a modeled number to record.
  • Program-lead voice. I owned definitions, rules, gates and the signature chain; engineers, SIs and vendor teams configured and built to the spec I signed. The only platform personally configured in canon is Manhattan DOM.
  • The pitch ends on the primary metric. Then stop. Silence after the pitch is the interviewer filling their STAR worksheet; their probes are the second half of the answer.
  • For Infrastructure specifically: speak in gates, invariants and triggers — the published ramp gate and the throttle built in advance; the six cutover invariants (Σ AFS conserved; no SKU sellable in two places; negatives cleared first); rollback triggers as numbers (sync < 99% for 4 h; p95 > 2× for 1 h); hypercare exit as a number (< 2 blockers/day sustained). A gate that cannot stop the program is decoration.
  • Asset utilization is the result frame: +15% units per labor-hour (signed), one pool instead of two (√2 pooling, modeled and labeled), self-transfers inside one roof eliminated, robotics at full volume by week three, $20M of pipeline unblocked on the date.
Loop-consistency rule. One question, one story; never the same story to two interviewers — interviewers compare notes. Held for other packets (do not spend here): S11/S12 (Tripathy) · S6/S8 (Hoerger) · S1/S3 (Lan) · S16/S15/S10/S14 (Josyula). v2 overlap: U1, U3 and U6 re-cut the AFS, merch-reserve and bucket-matrix facts that sit in Lan's S1 — the numbers are identical in both packets; answer from U1/U3/U6 only when the question is about the building, and say the program is the same. S5 (the architecture council) shares the UDC program's dated chain with S2 — Lan's backup pointer, not yours; S2 and S1 share program-level artifacts (issues log, rollback triggers) — if asked, say so.. If pushed for "another example" on an LP, use the named backup in the question bank or the backup bench at the end of this packet, never a story allocated to another interviewer.
How to read the "L6 level evidence" block under each story. Amazon's L6 functional bar (user-supplied rubric): Ambiguity — strategy or network undefined; I select the stakeholders, set the goals, deliver independently. Scope & influence — team-level scope; influences one to two levels up. Execution — tactical and strategic; simplifies or extends; applies best practices; force-multiplies; mitigates long-term risk; makes the short-versus-long-term trade-off explicit. Impact — long-term team goals. Process improvement — operational-excellence best practices institutionalized. Complex — visible risks, roadblocks and constraints mitigated through expertise, judgment and influence to consensus. Overview themes the block is tuned to: designing and modeling the supply chain rather than working in it; developing new processes for others to own; root-cause mechanisms that resolve trending issues at scale; recommendations to leadership that outline competing metrics. The block sits right after Results so the evidence is on the page when the probing starts.

NACF inbound bridge — one whiteboard for nine storiesNEW

Every story ends with one sentence that lands on NACF. The sentence rides on the inbound booklet’s frames — CAP-A doors, CAP-B UPH, CAP-C cube, CAP-D dwell, CAP-E peak buffer, INV-D transfer break-even — on the booklet’s own illustrative FC (120,000 units a day). Draw this chain first, then say which link the story’s constraint lives in.

FrameLinkFormula → substitutionBasisValue
ADoors120,000 ÷ 6 ÷ 40 = 500 pallets; ÷ 26 = 19.2 trailers/day; ÷ 10 h = 1.92/h; ÷ 0.5 trailer per door-hour = 3.84 doors; ÷ 0.85 = 4.5booklet CAP-A · illustrative FC5 doors
BUPH labor120,000 ÷ 300 = 400 h; × 1.15 = 460 paid h; 0.7 × 300 + 0.3 × 180 = 264 blended; 120,000 ÷ 264 = 454.5 hbooklet CAP-B · 300 UPH, 60% learning curve illustrative+13.6% hours
CCube2,000,000 × 0.25 = 500,000 ft³; 1,200,000 × 0.85 = 1,020,000 effective; 500,000 ÷ 1,020,000booklet CAP-C · 0.25 ft³/unit illustrative49.0% true use
DDwell2.0 ÷ (1 − 0.85) = 13.3 h; 2.0 ÷ (1 − 0.95) = 40.0 h; 40.0 ÷ 13.3 = 3.0×booklet CAP-D · qualitative queueing approximation85% → 95% ≈ 3× dwell
EPeak buffer156,000 ÷ 264 = 591 h; 591 − 454.5 = 136 h; ÷ 10 = 13.6booklet CAP-E · p90 156,000 illustrative≈14 flex heads
D′INV-D$3.00 ÷ $12 = 0.25 — transfer when stockout probability > 25%booklet INV-D · $3.00 transfer, $12 margin illustrativep* = 25%
Story → frame → the question I would ask at NACF
StoryWhat the node taughtThe NACF sentenceFrame
S2A published ramp gate and a dial the floor owns (94 vs 98 → ~30%).Which link binds this week at a new FC — doors at the 85% line, UPH after the learning-curve tax, or cube at 49% effective — and who owns the stop?A · B · C
S18Tolerance published before mock one; a freeze sized to the longest lane; a what-if for the single-point dependency.Publish the receipt-reconciliation tolerance before the first mock; hold 5 planned doors against 3.84 raw — about 1.2 doors of slack — so a day-one surprise does not move the date.A · D
U1Every event lands in exactly one bucket; zero or two is a defect.“Stranded” inventory is an event that landed in zero states. Build the receive → stow → sellable event matrix before an FC ramps.booklet “stranded”
U2Profile overlap, order shape and correlation before pooling is claimed.Do two FCs carry the same ASINs and see the same demand? Pooling pays only at low correlation; the physical move is INV-D — transfer when p > 25%.INV-D
U3Merch reserve is in the building and not promisable; p* = cost ÷ (gain + cost).Promise pickable, not received. Same break-even shape as INV-D (3 ÷ 12); 10,000 reserved units at 0.25 ft³ hold 2,500 ft³ of the 1,020,000 effective.C · D′
U4The constraint moved from labor hours to accuracy; the throttle cost ≈421 h.421 h is about 10.5 people for a week against CAP-B’s 13.6% learning tax and CAP-E’s ~14 flex heads — name the binding resource first.B · E
U5One irreversible instant; stop conditions written as numbers before the first task.The first live inbound appointment is the irreversible instant; the 85% door ceiling is the slack that absorbs a re-pointed or late trailer.A · D
U6A negative AFS decomposes to duplicate event, feed latency, override.Committed units above what the building can back: a double receive scan, dock-to-stock lag, a manual adjustment — zero-tolerance reconciliation of dock-received vs system-available.D
U7The issues log read severity-first, with the dock, power and footprint rows named.Launch register with the dock row at the top: appointments, placards, pallet-build positions; a SKU with no footprint defaults a slot, which is wasted cube at the 85% line.A · C

All booklet inputs are the booklet’s own illustrative FC; nothing here is a Sephora figure. The people-week conversion assumes a 40-hour week (421 ÷ 40 = 10.5).

The stories — business-first, canon numbers

Nine primaries on the Sephora distribution-center work: S2 and S18 (elevated) and seven new cuts of the same node facts, U1–U7. Each: 60-second pitch → Situation/Task → Action in gated phases → Results → L6 evidence → five facets and probing → the four-table math → Twist map → behavioral pack, angle cards, trap drills, 20-second version and NACF bridge.

S2 — Sephora Utah DC — two systemic warehouses in one building → one comingle pool, coupled with a WMS upgrade and a robotics ramp behind a published gate (+15% processing efficiency, zero customer-facing incidents)ELEVATED

LPs this story can serve here: Insist on the Highest Standards (primary — the 98% gate that fired at 94) · Deliver Results (two go-lives, peak untouched) · Have Backbone (the throttle) · Strive to be Earth's Best Employer (the floor protected during the ramp) · Bias for Action (the throttle decision the week the gate failed)
Process family: Make / Deliver — DC operating model (one inventory pool, three planning identities), automation ramp governance, staged go-lives, cutover runbook, hypercare exit
Honesty label. I owned discovery through FSD for the location and inventory model, the staging and the gates; BlueYonder, Locus and the platform teams built and configured; DC Ops ran the floor and measured the +15% post-stabilization. The +15% is Sephora's signed number; every dollar is my model on an illustrative 100K-units/week DC. Program-level artifacts (dated gate chain, 836-row issues log, rollback triggers) are shared with S1/S5 — one umbrella program.
Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each
Insist on the Highest Standards. I published the robotics gate — 98% pick-accurate — before the robots arrived; when week two read 94 it fired and the bots went to ~30% for a week.
Deliver Results. Two go-lives on their dates, each carrying one defect class, peak untouched, zero customer-facing incidents and +15% processing efficiency signed by Sephora.
Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit. I challenged the single-go-live instinct and held the comingle-reserve improvements out of the release to protect zero incidents — priced as a later enhancement.
Strive to be Earth’s Best Employer. The throttle and the DC-Ops-owned dial protected the floor’s throughput during the ramp; the interim reporting burden was named as the price. No headcount or attrition number is claimed.
60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Sephora's Utah DC ran as two systemic warehouses in one building — Dotcom 801 and Retail 901 — transferring stock to itself and paying duplicate handling for zero customer value. The ask was one omni comingle facility, with a BlueYonder WMS upgrade and Locus robotics on the same critical path, an October–December peak freeze, about a dozen impacted systems, 60+ stakeholders, and zero customer-facing incidents allowed. Task. I owned discovery through the signed design for the location and inventory model, the staging and the acceptance gates. Action. I redesigned the operating model as one physical pool with three planning identities, so each channel kept its forecasting lens without a second truth; split the program into two go-lives so each carried one defect class — January proved reserve rules against live demand with the floor untouched, May moved warehouse execution; published the robotics gate at 98% pick-accurate throughput with the throttle dial built in advance and owned by DC Ops; ran three mock cutovers against the 0.5% tolerance and a 343-task runbook whose one irreversible instant was zero-then-publish, so nothing was sellable in two places. When week two of the robotics ramp hit 94 against 98, the gate fired: bots throttled to about 30% of volume for a week, zone and slotting logic root-caused, full volume week three. Result. Primary metric: DC processing efficiency — baseline index 100 → 115, +15%, Sephora-signed, measured by DC Ops post-stabilization; zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives; rollback armed twice, never fired. About $1.6M a year modeled — labor at +15%, pooling carry, fewer split shipments, self-transfer handling removed — and about $2.2M of one-time safety stock released by pooling, also modeled; the +15% and the zero are the record.

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — The Utah DC ran as two systemic warehouses — Dotcom 801 (selling location 800) and Retail 901 — transferring stock to itself and paying duplicate handling for zero customer value. The ask: one omni/comingle facility, with a BlueYonder WMS 8.2 → 2023.1 upgrade and Locus robotics on the same critical path, about a dozen impacted systems, an Oct–Dec peak freeze, zero customer-facing incidents allowed.

The challenges sat on the critical path itself: the WMS upgrade and the robotics ramp shared it, the peak freeze bounded it, and the interim would run two live truths. The register's top three risks — two live truths during the interim, a robotics ramp with no stop condition, and a peak collision — each had a trigger, a mitigation and an owner before sign-off.

T — Discovery through FSD for the location and inventory model, the staging and the acceptance gates; 60+ stakeholders (~80 names across ~15 functions) on the steering path; none of the systems reported to me.

Baselines on the table: processing efficiency index 100 (units per labor-hour) · customer-facing incidents allowed: 0 · robotics gate 98% published in advance from the pre-ramp baseline and the incident budget · self-transfers and duplicate handling — the standing cost one pool exists to remove.

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery (3/29 → 4/30) — profiled 801/901 (SKU overlap, order profiles); sponsor objectives and success criteria — one omni facility, self-transfers eliminated, zero customer-facing incidents, peak untouched; systems-impacted table. Exit bar: every impacted system named to an owner with a written LOE (landed 7/2); criteria signed by steering.
  • Plan & Design (5/15 → 8/14) — council pack (three decisions + an out-of-scope fence); the 900/901 location/inventory model with three planning identities over one physical location; staged two-go-live timeline; RAID log; FSD 8/14. Exit bar: council sign-off 7/31; FSD 8/14; peak-freeze boundary decided; LOE-backed plan across ~30 build tracks.
  • Build & Test (Aug → Jan/Apr) — node × channel × bucket-transition matrix as the regression asset; dated E2E days — Inbound 11/22, Dotcom 12/20, Retail 1/30 — every function in one room; Locus gate definition (98%) with the throttle spec; three mock cutovers against the 0.5% tolerance (the S18 discipline reused); the Locus RTL slipped three weeks — escalated the week it surfaced, re-slotted openly, UAT not compressed to absorb it. Exit bar: E2E green on three dates; Blocker + Critical open = 0 at each go/no-go; mock 3 inside tolerance.
  • Cutover & Go-Live (January; May 1 → Jul 1) — runbook of 343 tasks / ~815 hours, each with owner, duration and predecessor; six-step May choreography — clear negatives, transfer AFS to Uncommitted, scan-and-reserve, zero-then-publish; rollback triggers per interface (sync < 99% for 4 h; order-flow p95 > 2× budget for 1 h). Exit bar: six invariants held (Σ AFS conserved; no SKU sellable on 800 and 900 at once; negatives cleared first); rollback never fired.
  • Hypercare / Close (Jun → Jul) — monthly arc of new issue rows 271 → 65 → 21 → 14 → 4 (June → October); Locus weekly gate read — week 2 at 94 vs 98 → throttle to ~30% → RCA (zone/slotting logic) → full volume week 3; hypercare exit memo; template handed to the next DC. Exit bar: < 2 blockers/day sustained → exit week 3 of 4; +15% measured post-stabilization by DC Ops.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the location/inventory model, the staging, the gates, the council pack, the runbook and the go/no-go recommendation. BlueYonder, Locus and the platform teams built and configured; DC Ops ran the floor, owned the robotics gate and the dial before the ramp — so when it fired, the rule fired; I did not improvise. Digital worried a virtual Retail DC would blur their forecasting lens — the three-identity design made the concern the design rather than an argument. The comingle-reserve improvements were held out of the release to protect zero incidents, priced as a later enhancement at steering.

Designed the process; others own it now. I designed the location/inventory model, the gates and the throttle dial; DC Ops owned the dial before the ramp and runs the floor on it, and the hypercare template went to the next program. Trade-off put to leadership. The recommendation to leadership outlined the competing metrics — +15% processing efficiency and the robotics ramp vs zero customer-facing incidents and peak untouched — and made the gate (98%) and the throttle (~30%) the published answer before the robots arrived.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality (canon numbers only)
  • $ — ≈$1.6M/yr modeled (labor at +15% ≈$0.88M · pooling carry ≈$0.22M · fewer split shipments ≈$0.45M · self-transfer handling ≈$0.10M) + ≈$2.2M one-time safety-stock release modeled via √2 pooling; the +15% is Sephora's signed number; every dollar is my model on an illustrative 100K-units/week DC (range $1.1–2.4M/yr).
  • # — cutover runbook 343 tasks / 48,885 task-minutes ≈ 815 hrs; issues log 836 rows / 85.4% closed; monthly new-row arc 271 → 65 → 21 → 14 → 4 (record, from the log).
  • % — processing efficiency +15% (record, Sephora-signed); robotics gate 98%, week 2 at 94, bots throttled to ~30% of volume for a week, full volume week 3 (record).
  • Time — council 5/15 → sign-off 7/31 → FSD 8/14; E2E 11/22 · 12/20 · 1/30; January and May go-lives; hypercare exited week 3 of 4 (record).
  • Customer — zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives (record) — the constraint every other number moved inside.
  • Quality — six cutover invariants held; rollback armed for both go-lives, never fired; peak untouched (record).

Probing — five facets, then the hardest follow-upsNEW
The five facets — where each one lives in this story
1 · Data analysis. Grain: node × week × bot share; SKU × pool. Data: both books profiled in discovery (U2), labor hours, picks attempted/accurate, the 836-row issues log, the 343-task runbook. Cuts: accuracy by week; new rows by month (271 → 4). Revealed: the week-2 miss was zone/slotting logic, and the case rested on recurring rows more than on pooling.
2 · Solution design. Options: (A) keep two warehouses and reconcile; (B) one pool, one blended planning lens; (C) one physical pool, three planning identities (chosen); and a staging choice — one go-live vs two (chosen: two, each carrying one defect class). Why: C keeps Digital’s forecasting lens without a second truth; two go-lives keep diagnosis univariate.
3 · Math & data logic. +15% → hours 240,000 / 1.15 = 208,700 (−13.0%); pooling 1 − 1/√2 = 29.3% on $7.5M → $2.2M (bounded in U2); gate T = accurate ÷ attempted ≥ 98%; throttle 100% → 30% (bounded in U4); AFS conserved (U5).
4 · Implementation. Five gated phases (3/29 → 4/30 → 5/15 → 7/31 → 8/14 → E2E 11/22 · 12/20 · 1/30 → January → May 1 → Jul 1 → hypercare); owners: me (model, staging, gates, go/no-go), BlueYonder/Locus/platform (build), DC Ops (floor, gate, dial). Controls: Blocker + Critical = 0, mock 3 ≤ 0.5%, rollback triggers.
5 · Probe questions. The six answered above in the Probing block; the zoom-ins that stand alone are U1 (matrix), U2 (profile), U3 (reserve), U4 (capacity), U5 (runbook), U6 (negatives).
Facet 5 — probe questions (challenge Q&A tuned to this interviewer)

Infra — Why 98%, and what did you actually do at 94?

98% was set from the pre-ramp baseline and the incident budget before the event, and DC Ops owned it. At 94 in week two I throttled the bots to about 30% of volume for a week, root-caused the zone and slotting logic, and ramped again — full volume by week three. A gate that cannot stop the program is decoration; mine did.

Infra — Why two go-lives instead of one big one?

So we never hunted two defect classes in the same week. January proved reserve rules against live demand while the floor was untouched; May left warehouse execution as the only remaining class. One go-live would have put a reserve defect and a floor defect in the same room.

Infra — What made you say go in May?

Counted rows, not the room: Blocker + Critical at zero, mock 3 inside the 0.5% tolerance, the 343-task runbook rehearsed, rollback armed per interface. I would have said no-go on one open Blocker; I never had to.

Infra — What were the rollback triggers and did any fire?

Sync below 99% for four consecutive hours, or order-flow p95 above twice budget for one hour — rollback per interface behind them. Armed for both go-lives; never fired. The one irreversible instant was step 4, zero-then-publish, so no SKU was ever sellable in two places.

Infra — The labor number is the biggest piece of your $1.6M. How firm?

The fifteen percent is measured and signed; the 120 FTE and the $28 loaded rate are my assumptions. Pull the DC's direct hours for the two quarters before and after and multiply by payroll — that turns the model into a record in an afternoon. If the split-shipment row didn't move, the total is about $1.1M.

Infra — What would you do differently?

Staff the interim reporting workstream on day one, not month three — for four months every report had to declare which system of record it read. And the Locus slip: escalated the week it surfaced, but I would have put the RTL on the register as a risk, not a task, from the start.

Spoken statementNEW

“Conservatively, about one point six million a year: roughly nine hundred thousand of labor, because the same volume takes thirteen percent fewer hours at plus fifteen percent efficiency; about two hundred thousand a year of carry on the two point two million of safety stock one pool releases versus two; about four hundred fifty thousand from fewer split shipments; and about a hundred thousand from the DC no longer shipping to itself. The plus fifteen percent and the zero incidents are measured and signed by Sephora; every dollar figure is my model on an illustrative hundred-thousand-units-a-week DC, and I will tell you which input each one rests on.”

If challengedNEW

“The labor number is the biggest — how firm?”

The fifteen percent is measured; the headcount and the $28 loaded rate are my assumptions. Pull the DC’s actual direct hours for the two quarters before and after and multiply by payroll — that turns the model into a record in one afternoon.

“Pooling sounds like a textbook claim.”

It is, and I labeled it. √2 pooling assumes two roughly equal independent pools on the same SKUs. Measure SKU-level on-hand on the last day of 801 versus the first stable month of 901; the release is whatever the difference actually is. U2 bounds it at about $0.74M–$2.2M.

“Split shipments — evidence?”

The softest row. Parcels per dotcom order from the ship system before and after May, times the carrier’s per-parcel cost. If the point didn’t move, I drop the row and the total is about $1.1M.

Where this lands at Amazon. An FC launch/retrofit with automation on the critical path: publish the ramp gate and the throttle before the robots arrive, stage go-lives so each carries one defect class, hold peak sacred, and write the rollback as a number. Zero-then-publish is the same discipline as never letting one unit be promisable from two nodes at once. Asset utilization (+15% units per labor-hour; one pool), reliability (zero incidents, rollback never fired).

Behavioral pack — four separate 90-second STAR answers, each from a different moment of the storyNEW
Insist on the Highest Standards · AIQB Q4Q1. “Tell me about a time when you had to make a decision between standards and delivery.”

I published the standard before there was any pressure to waive it. The robots were arriving at the Utah DC, and I wrote the gate — 98% pick-accurate completed over attempted — into the council pack, with a throttle dial and the weekly read, and DC Ops owned the gate and the dial before the ramp. A gate published before the pressure exists is most of the holding. Week two read 94. The floor owner’s own rule fired and I throttled the bots to about 30% of volume for a week — a week of robot volume traded for a root-caused ramp. The zone and slotting logic was the cause; it was fixed, and week three ran at full volume. Processing efficiency ended at +15%, signed by Sephora and measured by DC Ops after stabilization. A gate that cannot stop the program is decoration; this one did.

Number to land: 98 published · 94 read · ~30% throttle · full volume week 3  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF I would publish the ramp gate for a new FC — the door ceiling, the UPH floor — before the first trailer, with the dial owned by the building.  ·  Do NOT say: that the robots failed; that I fixed the slotting logic.
Deliver Results · AIQB Q3Q2. “Tell me about a time when you not only met a goal but considerably exceeded expectations.”

The constraint was zero customer-facing incidents, so I designed the program to carry one defect class at a time. About a dozen impacted systems — with a BlueYonder upgrade and Locus robotics on the same critical path and an October-to-December peak freeze. I split the work into two go-lives: January was the planning go-live — purchasing and inbound, with the reserve rules live against demand — and the floor untouched; May moved warehouse execution. Each go-live was a counted decision: Blocker plus Critical at zero, mock three inside the 0.5% tolerance, a 343-task runbook rehearsed, rollback armed. Both landed on their dates with zero customer-facing incidents, peak untouched, hypercare exited in week three of four, and +15% processing efficiency signed by Sephora. The modeled value is about $1.6 million a year; the +15% and the zero are the record.

Number to land: 2 go-lives · zero incidents · +15% signed  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF a new FC launch has the same shape: stage it so each step carries one risk class, and decide each step on counted rows.  ·  Do NOT say: that the $1.6M is signed; that I ran the floor.
Earn Trust · AIQB Q1Q3. “Tell me about a time when you had to communicate a change in direction that you anticipated people would have concerns with.”

Digital worried that a virtual Retail DC would blur their forecasting lens, and they were right to worry. I did not argue them out of it; I made the concern the design: one physical location with three planning identities, so each channel keeps its lens without a second truth. Across sixty-plus stakeholders I owned none of the systems, so I wrote the success criteria with the sponsor — one omni facility, self-transfers eliminated, zero customer-facing incidents, peak untouched — and steering, meaning DC Ops, IT and Supply Chain Strategy, signed them. DC Ops owned the robotics gate and the dial before the ramp, so when it fired the floor’s own rule fired. The build tracks were slotted by business criticality times effort under one issues log. Trust came from putting the concern into the design and the stop condition into the hands of the people who carry the consequence.

Number to land: 60+ stakeholders · 3 planning identities · gate and dial owned by DC Ops  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF, placement and inbound changes cross many orgs I would not own; I would convert objections into design rows the same way.  ·  Do NOT say: that Digital agreed with everything; I know their concern, not their private view.
Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit · AIQB Q2Q4. “Describe a time when you took an unpopular stance in a meeting with peers and your leader.”

The prevailing instinct was one go-live: one cutover, one date, done. I challenged it. One go-live would have put a reserve-rule defect and a floor-execution defect in the same room the same week, and with sixty-plus stakeholders and zero tolerance for customer-facing incidents that is a diagnosis problem, not a schedule problem. So I proposed two go-lives so we never hunted two defect classes in one week — January for the reserve rules against live demand, May for execution — and I also held the comingle-reserve improvements out of the release, priced as a later enhancement at steering, to protect the zero. Both landed on their dates. The cost was a second cutover and the discipline of saying no to a good idea at the wrong time; the benefit was single-cause diagnosis.

Number to land: 2 go-lives, 1 defect class each; improvements held out  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF I would stage a launch the same way and hold good ideas for the next release.  ·  Do NOT say: that two go-lives were free; the second cutover has a price.

Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups, with numeric answersNEW
How do you know the +15% came from the consolidation and not from the robots or the WMS upgrade?

I don’t split it, and Sephora’s number doesn’t either: +15% is whole-program processing efficiency (units per labor-hour), measured by DC Ops post-stabilization. The consolidation, the WMS upgrade and the robotics were on one critical path by design. Attribution among them would need a before/after on lanes that did not get robots.

Say: “The +15% is the program’s, signed and measured by DC Ops; I would not attribute it to one lever, and the number that would let me is a lane-level before/after.”

What was the counterfactual?

Two warehouses transferring stock to themselves (≈260,000 units a year on paper) and two safety-stock pools — the cost of not consolidating. The counterfactual dollars are modeled; the record is the efficiency and the zero incidents.

Say: “The counterfactual is modeled; what I can prove is +15% measured by DC Ops and zero customer-facing incidents.”

What did you give up?

The comingle-reserve improvements (priced as a later enhancement), a week of robot volume (the throttle), and for four months a clean reporting picture (the interim). Each was a deliberate trade for zero incidents.

Say: “Three things, on purpose: the reserve improvements, a week of robot volume, and four months of cleaner reporting — in exchange for zero customer-facing incidents.”

20-second version + NACF bridgeNEW

20-second version. At Sephora’s Utah DC I merged two systemic warehouses into one comingle pool — three planning identities over one location — through two go-lives and a robotics ramp behind a published 98% gate that fired at 94: +15% processing efficiency signed by Sephora, zero customer-facing incidents, rollback armed twice and never fired.

NACF bridge. NACF capacity chain: one node, one published ramp gate and an owned dial. A new FC’s inbound ramp is CAP-A doors held at an 85% ceiling, CAP-B UPH with the learning-curve tax (+13.6% hours), CAP-C cube read at 49% effective — and the same rule that the people who own the building own the stop condition.

S18 — Sephora — legacy WMS → JDA WMS dual-DC migration: a 0.5% reconciliation tolerance published before mock one, three mocks read by cause class (7% → 1.8% → 0.3%), and the RF-device miss ownedELEVATED

Process family: Make / Deliver — warehouse execution system cutover across two DCs; inventory-accuracy governance; freeze, failback and hypercare discipline

SOURCE CONFLICT — the RF-device miss (read before you say it). Master canon (S18): ~100 of ~400 RF devices failed the conversion script at cutover; a rehearsed paper-pick fallback fired; ≈$45K fallback against ≈$875K of slip. Apple v11, chapter G2 (from your 2022 failure notes): the upgrade script failed on refreshed RF hardware in a device test scheduled days before training; manual conversion could not finish under COVID night shifts; training slipped 1.5 weeks; the notes record ~400 devices in one telling and ~100 in another — “say hundreds in the room.” Precedence applied (source docs, then Apple v11, then master): every line I added to S18 says “hundreds”, “the pre-training device test”, “training +1.5 weeks, the date held”, and none says the paper-pick $45K or the ≈19× ratio. The S18 body above has been brought to the Apple G2 wording for the RF-device miss; the master’s paper-pick and ~19× lines are not carried forward. Common ground in every version: the miss was mine, scored by effort not impact, training slipped 1.5 weeks, the date held, ~3% under budget. 60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Two Sephora DCs ran on a legacy WMS with no documentation, an eight-month hard deadline, a ~$1.8M budget, and a $20M ROI pipeline of four programs gated behind go-live — about $2.5M a month of slip on average (my division of the $20M, not banked). Inventory accuracy across two DCs could not wobble at cutover, and I had never run a WMS migration. Task. Own the full lifecycle — charter, design sign-off, the ERP ↔ WMS ↔ 3PL ↔ carrier interface inventory, UAT, cutover, hypercare — accountable to the sponsor and Finance. Action. I wrote the success criteria in week one — on the date, inside a published reconciliation tolerance, zero accuracy incidents, a numeric hypercare exit — and learned the floor in month one until I could walk receive-to-ship unaided. I built the interface inventory from message logs with an owner and a test cell per contract, set the mapping-table standard as data not code, and published the 0.5% tolerance with Finance before mock one so the number belonged to us before the test. Three mocks were read by cause class, not headline: 7% (units of measure, location names, status vocabularies) → 1.8% (in-transit timing) → 0.3%. Cutover ran a 72-hour in-transit freeze with the legacy system read-only for 30 days. When a device test before training showed hundreds of RF devices failing the conversion script, inventory was inside tolerance and the failure was device conversion, not data — so I held the date, let the training criterion slip 1.5 weeks, and told leadership the same day. Result. Primary metric: inventory reconciliation variance at cutover — 7% → 1.8% → 0.3% against the 0.5% tolerance. On time, about 3% under the ~$1.8M budget (~$54K), the $20M pipeline unblocked the next month — slip avoided, not banked. Hypercare exited week three at under two blockers a day. The RF miss is mine.

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — Two DCs on a legacy WMS; zero inherited documentation; a hard eight-month deadline with a $20M ROI pipeline of four programs gated behind go-live (~$2.5M/month of slip — my division, modeled); budget ~$1.8M; I had never run a WMS migration. Inventory accuracy across two DCs could not wobble at cutover.

The register at design sign-off: undocumented interface surprises, conversion mismatch above tolerance, and 3PL/carrier freeze non-compliance — each with a trigger, a data-first mitigation and an owner. The one I missed: RF device conversion sat on the log as a task, not a risk.

T — Own the full lifecycle — discovery charter, design sign-off, the ERP ↔ WMS ↔ 3PL ↔ carrier interface inventory, UAT, cutover, hypercare — accountable to the sponsor and Finance. Team: 14 core + 6 DC Ops.

Baselines on the table: conversion mismatch — gated at the 0.5% tolerance signed with Finance before mock 1 (mock 1 opened at 7%) · accuracy incidents allowed: zero · the date — eight months, hard · budget ~$1.8M · hypercare exit < 2 blockers/day.

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery (month 1) — sponsor charter in week one (objectives, assumptions, success criteria, phase entry/exit criteria); interface inventory v0 from message logs; systems-impacted table with LOE per owner; the floor walked until I could follow receive → put-away → pick → ship unaided. Exit bar: charter signed by sponsor and Finance; every interface has a named owner.
  • Plan & Design (months 2–3) — design sign-off pack; interface inventory v1 with a test cell per contract; conversion approach — masters → open orders → on-hand → in-transit as its own batch; mapping-table standard (data, not code); the 0.5% tolerance published with Finance before mock 1. Exit bar: council sign-off; tolerance signed; stories slotted by business criticality × effort.
  • Build & Test (months 4–7) — QA demo schedule to DC Ops; UAT entrance memo (function testing done, conversion build done, test data handed off, pre-prod + perf ready) — QA could fail a build; three mock cutovers with written root causes by class: 7% → 1.8% → 0.3%; after the RF miss, the training plan and device-readiness table. Exit bar: mock 3 ≤ 0.5% → written go-live recommendation; training sign-off (slipped 1.5 weeks, date held).
  • Cutover & Go-Live (month 8) — runbook; 72-hour transfer freeze (≥ the longest lane transit); conversion batches; post-load reconciliation; legacy read-only switch; the go/no-go call. the RF-device conversion — hundreds of devices caught in the pre-training device test — had already moved training 1.5 weeks while the date held; leadership was told the same day with the slip priced. Exit bar: post-load mismatch inside tolerance; ship confirms posting; zero accuracy incidents.
  • Hypercare / Close (4 weeks planned) — blockers/day read daily; 30-day failback watch (legacy read-only); budget closed; exit memo with the single-point-dependency lesson. Exit bar: < 2 blockers/day sustained → exit week 3; ~3% under budget; pipeline programs started the next month.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the charter, the entrance/exit criteria, the interface inventory, the conversion approach, the mapping-table standard, the tolerance, the go/no-go recommendation and the fallback call; the team built, converted, tested and ran the floor; Finance co-owned the tolerance. Because the success criteria were written in week one and signed by sponsor and Finance, nobody negotiated the exit later. The RF miss is mine — I scored the task by effort when impact was what mattered — and it went upstairs the same afternoon with my name on the cause.

Designed the process; others own it now. I designed the charter, the tolerance, the entrance criteria and the mapping-table standard; the team and Finance now run cutovers against them, and the standard outlived the program. Trade-off put to leadership. The recommendation to the sponsor outlined the competing metrics — the eight-month date and the $20M pipeline behind it vs training readiness and conversion variance — and held the date while training slipped 1.5 weeks and the 0.5% tolerance did not move.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality (canon numbers only)
  • $ — ~3% under the ~$1.8M budget (~$54K, record); the $20M ROI pipeline unblocked the next month — ~$2.5M/month of slip (my division, modeled; not banked); ≈$875K modeled price of a 1.5-week go-live slip (my division of the $20M; not banked); ≈$0.1M modeled less inventory in dispute at 0.3% vs 0.5% on an illustrative $50M on-hand.
  • # — hundreds of RF devices failed the conversion script in the pre-training device test (a three-to-four-day estimate that proved wrong); team 14 core + 6 DC Ops across two DCs (record).
  • % — mock variance 7% → 1.8% → 0.3% against the 0.5% tolerance published with Finance before mock 1 (record).
  • Time — eight-month deadline held; 72-hour in-transit freeze; legacy read-only for 30 days; training slipped 1.5 weeks while the date did not move; hypercare exited week 3 of 4 (record).
  • Customer — zero accuracy incidents at cutover; ship confirms posting; post-load mismatch inside tolerance (record exit bars).
  • Quality — mocks read by cause class, each removing a named defect category; failback rehearsed, never used; a what-if plan now required for every single-point dependency (record).

Probing — five facets, then the hardest follow-upsNEW
The five facets — where each one lives in this story
1 · Data analysis. Grain: SKU × location × unit of measure; units in flight by lane. Data: legacy WMS and JDA conversion output, message logs for the interface inventory, three mock result sets. Cuts: variance by cause class (UoM, location names, status vocabularies, in-transit timing). Revealed: one mapping class — UoM, factor 12 on ~1% of SKUs — dominated the 7%.
2 · Solution design. Options: (A) big-bang cutover with tolerance TBD; (B) tolerance published before mock one, mocks read by cause class, freeze and failback (chosen); (C) parallel run. Chose B — the tolerance belongs to Finance and me before the test, not after.
3 · Math & data logic. M = Σ|Δ| / Σ ERP on-hand; 7.0% → 1.8% → 0.3% ≤ 0.5%; freeze F = 72 h ≥ max lane transit; slip price $2.5M / 30 = $83,333/day; 10.5 days → $875K (price of a 1.5-week go-live slip).
4 · Implementation. Five gated phases over eight months: charter week one; interface inventory with a test cell per contract; three mocks; 72-hour freeze; legacy read-only 30 days; device-readiness in training entrance criteria; hypercare exit week 3 at < 2 blockers/day.
5 · Probe questions. The six answered above in the Probing block, and the three trap drills below.
Facet 5 — probe questions (challenge Q&A tuned to this interviewer)

Infra — Why publish a tolerance before you knew you could hit it?

So the number belonged to Finance and me before the test, not after. Mock one's 7% was the price; mock three's 0.3% was worth it — and the tolerance was never renegotiated.

Infra — How do you read a mock?

Never by the headline alone. Every delta got a root-cause class — units of measure, location drift, status vocabularies, then in-transit timing. If the total shrank without a named cause, the mock was not evidence. 7 → 1.8 → 0.3 each removed a category, and the fixes were mapping tables as data, not code.

Infra — Hundreds of RF devices failed the device test before training. Why not move the date?

Because inventory was inside tolerance — the failure was device conversion, not data — and the training criterion could move without moving the go-live. I held the date, let training slip 1.5 weeks, and took the miss upstairs the same afternoon with my name on the cause.

Infra — What was the freeze and why 72 hours?

A 72-hour in-transit freeze, set at or above the longest lane transit, so in-transit could be converted as its own batch with Σ legacy = Σ JDA — the class that took mock two from 1.8% to 0.3%. Legacy stayed read-only for 30 days as the failback.

Infra — The $875K avoided slip — are you banking it?

No. It is modeled arithmetic — 10.5 days at about $83K a day, from my division of the $20M over eight months — on a slip that did not happen; it is the price that made holding the date the obvious call. What I would measure is the pipeline's first program start against plan, which was the next month.

Infra — Lesson, and what rule did you leave behind?

Any single-point dependency in front of an exit criterion gets a what-if plan regardless of estimated effort. Differently: dual-path device conversion on day one, and a device model × OS × script pass/fail table before training week.

Spoken statementNEW

“The record numbers first: on time, about $54K under a $1.8M budget, and the $20M pipeline started the next month, where every month of slip was worth about $2.5M on average — my division of the twenty million over eight months, not banked. A slip of the 1.5 weeks the training criterion moved would, had it moved the go-live, have cost about $875K — so I held the date and moved training, and said so straight. The modeled part is smaller and I’ll label it: landing at 0.3 percent against a 0.5 tolerance meant about $100K less inventory in dispute at cutover on an illustrative $50M on-hand, and at mock one’s 7 percent that would have been $3.5M. The date, the budget, the variance and the pipeline are measured; the on-hand value is my model.”

If challengedNEW

“Where does $50M of on-hand come from?”

It’s illustrative, two million units at $25, chosen so the percentages become dollars; the record gives me 0.3 percent, not the balance sheet. I’d firm it up with the actual ERP on-hand valuation at the freeze snapshot, which Finance already had.

“The $875K avoided slip is a counterfactual.”

Yes — record arithmetic on a slip that did not happen, so I never bank it; it is the price that made the fallback the obvious call. What I’d measure is the pipeline’s first program start against plan, which was the next month.

“What did the RF miss actually cost?”

The record is the 1.5 weeks of training sign-off — SME windows were scarce — and a go-live date that held. I have not priced a fallback and I will not invent a number; the master packet carries a modeled paper-pick figure that my 2022 notes do not, so I do not say it.

Where this lands at Amazon. Cutover discipline in its most literal form — a tolerance published with Finance before the first rehearsal, mocks read by cause class, a freeze sized to the longest lane, a tested failback, a numeric hypercare exit nobody can negotiate, and a rehearsed contingency that fired on the day. Reliability (0.3% against 0.5%, zero accuracy incidents), responsiveness (the decision the same day), cost efficiency (~3% under), the $20M pipeline on the date.

Behavioral pack — four separate 90-second STAR answers, each from a different moment of the storyNEW
Ownership · AIQB Q2Q1. “Describe a time when you didn't think you were going to meet a commitment you promised.”

The miss was mine, and I said so straight. The devices team called the RF conversion three to four days and straightforward, and I planned it that way — low effort, low attention. Just to be sure, I scheduled a device test with engineering days before training, and the upgrade script failed: the refreshed RF hardware did not run the old code. Hundreds of devices, and with key people on COVID night shifts the manual conversion could not finish in time, so I told leadership straight: training sign-off, a go-live exit criterion, slips a week and a half. The root cause was my risk model — I scored the task by effort, not impact, so a single-point dependency in front of an exit criterion had no what-if plan. The date held and the migration landed on time, about 3% under budget. The rule I left behind: every cross-functional dependency gets a what-if and a mitigation whatever the effort estimate, and device readiness moved into training’s entrance criteria — proven devices, not promised ones.

Number to land: training +1.5 weeks · date held · ~3% under budget  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF a single-point dependency in front of a launch — one carrier, one door, one scanner model — gets a what-if plan regardless of size.  ·  Do NOT say: an exact device count — say ‘hundreds’ (the 2022 notes record ~400 in one telling and ~100 in another); that the vendor script was the cause.
Bias for Action · AIQB Q4Q2. “Tell me about a time when you had to gather information and respond immediately to a situation.”

I ran a check I did not have to run, and it failed. Training sign-off depended on hundreds of RF guns, printers and workstations converting, and the devices team said a few days. Just to be sure, I scheduled a device test with engineering days before training. The script failed on the refreshed hardware. The decision that mattered was fast because the options fit on one page: manual conversion on COVID night shifts could not finish before training, so something had to move, and the choice was which date. Training moved a week and a half; the go-live did not — and I told leadership that straight, with my name on the cause. Then I changed the system: device readiness went into training’s entrance criteria, and the habit generalized into dress rehearsals for everything on the critical path — on UDC it became the RF-device proof-of-concept before cutover week.

Number to land: training +1.5 weeks; go-live date held  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF a rehearsal a week before the first live trailer is how a launch absorbs a surprise on the day — I would rehearse the dependency, not just the plan.  ·  Do NOT say: that I improvised; or a fallback or dollar figure the 2022 notes do not carry.
Learn and Be Curious · AIQB Q2Q3. “Describe a time when you took on work outside of your comfort area. How did you identify what you needed to learn to be successful?”

I had never run a WMS migration, and the deadline was eight months. I wrote the charter in week one — success criteria, assumptions, entry and exit criteria — so the destination existed before my knowledge did. Then I spent month one on the floor, walking receive, put-away, pick and ship until I could follow a unit through the building unaided, and reading the message logs to build the interface inventory with an owner and a test cell per contract. The mocks were where I proved the learning: each delta got a root-cause class — units of measure, location names, status vocabularies, in-transit timing — so I was learning by removing categories, not by reading documentation. By the third mock the variance was 0.3% against a 0.5% tolerance. I did not claim to configure the system; the team converted and built.

Number to land: month 1 on the floor; 7% → 1.8% → 0.3%  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF I would walk the dock-to-stock path in my first weeks before proposing a change to it.  ·  Do NOT say: that I configured JDA or converted the data myself.
Insist on the Highest Standards · AIQB Q1Q4. “Describe a time when you refused to compromise your standards around quality/customer service, etc.”

I published the reconciliation tolerance — 0.5% — with Finance before the first mock. So the number belonged to Finance and to me before the test, not after. Mock one came in at 7% — fourteen times the bar — and that was the price. I did not renegotiate; I read the mock by cause class: units of measure were the dominant class — a case booked as twelve eaches is eleven units of error — then location names and status vocabularies. The fix was mapping tables as data, not code. Mock two: 1.8%. Mock three: a 72-hour in-transit freeze and in-transit converted as its own batch, 0.3%. The written go recommendation came off mock three, inside the tolerance. The tolerance was never renegotiated, and the exit bar for hypercare — under two blockers a day — was in the charter in week one, so nobody negotiated that either.

Number to land: 0.5% published first; 7.0% → 1.8% → 0.3%  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF I would publish the reconciliation tolerance — dock-received vs system-available — before the first mock receipt.  ·  Do NOT say: that 7% was a surprise to Finance; they signed the bar first.
Same story, three angles — data · conflict/influence/backbone · failure-learningNEW
Data angle · Dive Deep“Seven percent, one point eight, point three — each mock removed a named category.”
Headline metric: M = 140,000 / 2,000,000 = 7.0% → 0.3%
Mocks read by cause class: UoM (12 → 11 units of error per case) first, then in-transit timing as its own batch. If the total shrank without a named cause the mock was not evidence. Units are illustrative; the percentages are the record.		Conflict / influence angle · Insist on the Highest Standards“I published a tolerance with Finance before I knew I could hit it.”
Headline metric: 0.5% never renegotiated
Finance co-owned the number; the sponsor signed the charter in week one; leadership was told the same day the training criterion slipped 1.5 weeks — the date held, the tolerance did not move.		Failure / learning angle · Ownership“A three-day device task broke the training gate, and I had scored it by effort.”
Headline metric: training +1.5 weeks; date held; ~3% under
Impact, not effort, is the score; a single-point dependency in front of an exit criterion gets a what-if plan. The test I ran just to be sure saved the go-live, not the training gate. Differently: a device model × OS × script pass/fail table at refresh. The rule shows in the mocks: 7.0% → 1.8% → 0.3%.
Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups, with numeric answersNEW
How do you know the three mocks measured the same thing?

Same formula, same denominator (Σ ERP on-hand), same scope; the changes between mocks were named cause-class fixes. Units are illustrative; the percentages are the record.

Say: “Same formula and denominator each time, and every change between mocks was a named cause class — if the total shrank without a named cause I did not count it.”

“A week and a half on training — that is a small failure. Give me a bigger one.”

Fair. The bigger one is the Elementum UAT that crashed (Apple G1) — a lost sign-off. I keep this one because its lesson transfers best: the failure class that hits mature programs is not the big risk everyone watches, it is the three-day task nobody did a what-if on. The size of the slip is not the point; the blind spot is.

Say: “A week and a half is small; the blind spot is not — impact decides the attention a task gets, never its effort estimate.”

What is the number that would have changed your mind on go?

Mock three above 0.5%, a failed freeze or a failback I could not describe per interface. The RF failure was a different class: it moved training, not the go.

Say: “Mock three over half a percent — that single number was the go/no-go.”

20-second version + NACF bridgeNEW

20-second version. At Sephora I took two DCs from a legacy WMS to JDA in eight months, ~3% under a ~$1.8M budget, by publishing a 0.5% reconciliation tolerance with Finance before mock one and reading three mocks by cause — 7%, 1.8%, 0.3% — and when the RF device conversion failed its pre-training test I told leadership straight — training slips a week and a half, the date holds — and made what-if plans a rule.

NACF bridge. NACF’s inbound ramp is a migration of a different kind — a new FC’s dock, UPH and cube all go live together — and the same discipline applies: publish the tolerance before the first mock, size the freeze to the longest lane, rehearse the fallback, and give single-point dependencies a what-if. CAP-A’s 5 doors at an 85% ceiling are the slack that lets a day-one surprise be absorbed without moving the date.

U1 — The node × channel × bucket-transition matrix — one regression asset that proved every physical move on the floor lands in exactly one system bucketNEW

LPs this story serves here: Invent and Simplify (primary — one rule, one asset) · Dive Deep (primary — event traces, the 836-row log) · Insist on the Highest Standards (what “tested” means) · Earn Trust (QA demoed to DC Ops in build) · Success and Scale (backup — a new node is a row, not a project)
Process family: Make / Deliver — test and acceptance design for a comingled node; physical-event ↔ system-bucket integrity; regression asset reused through hypercare and the monthly release train
Honesty label. I owned the matrix design and its assertions as program owner of the location/inventory model; BlueYonder, ISM and platform teams built to it, QA ran it and owned the gate, DC Ops confirmed in UAT. The rule, the test families, the entry gate and the issues-log numbers are record. The cell counts (6 node-channel pairs, 14 events, 61 live cells) are illustrative VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH — confirm the real matrix size before saying a count aloud. The ISM/S4 bucket formula is the same program artifact as Lan's S1; here it is told from the floor-event side. Never claim configuring S/4, ISM or BlueYonder.
Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each
Invent and Simplify. About a dozen impacted systems and about thirty build tracks ran on one rule — every event maps to exactly one bucket — and one entry gate: no story enters QA without a matrix cell reference.
Dive Deep. I read one SKU, one day, event by event: an event landing in zero buckets promises 3 units that are gone (560), in two buckets hides 3 (554); the right answer is 557.
Insist on the Highest Standards. “Tested” meant every live cell asserted before/after with numbers, three dated E2E days with every function in one room, and Blocker + Critical open = 0 at each go/no-go.
Earn Trust. QA demoed each matrix family to DC Ops and planners during build, so UAT was confirmation, not discovery.
60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. At the Utah DC one physical pool had to serve three node types and two channels, and “available” had to move correctly on every physical event — a store ring, a pick, a pack, a ship confirm, a receipt, a transfer, an adjustment — across about a dozen impacted systems and about thirty build tracks, with zero customer-facing incidents allowed. Task. I owned the node × channel × bucket-transition matrix: the design and the regression asset the whole program tested against. Action. Discovery: a bucket inventory with exactly one owning system per bucket, and an event inventory — POS flash sale, omni pending, ship confirm, the S/4 Aggregation# posting, and SAP movement types 101, 311, 601, 701 and 702. Plan & Design: one rule — every event maps to exactly one bucket; a reversal must reference its original; a replayed event is a no-op. Build & Test: every cell carries a before/after assertion with numbers, and no story enters QA without a cell reference; three dated E2E days ran the whole flow in one room. Cutover: January and May go/no-go off counted rows — Blocker + Critical open = 0. Hypercare: ISM-vs-MB52 drift at zero SKUs on consecutive days; four in-transit cells and a de-aggregation family added from what the floor taught us. Result. Primary metric: customer-facing incidents — zero through both go-lives, with 836 issues logged, 441 of them Blocker or Critical (52.8%), 85.4% closed. The dollars are a what-if, labeled: break-even is about 1.8 escaped cell-classes a year.

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — One physical pool, three node types (Store, Retail DC, Dotcom DC), three working definitions of “available,” and a physical floor that generates events the books must follow: pick reserve, ecom pick and pack, ship confirm, receipt, transfer, adjustment. Left to the happy-path E2E, an event can land in zero buckets (oversell) or two (undersell) and nobody sees it until a customer does.

T — Own the design and the regression asset: which events exist, which bucket each one moves, what the numbers must be before and after, and the gate that stops an unproven story from entering QA. None of the systems reported to me; ~30 build tracks ran under one issues log; stories were slotted by business criticality × effort.

Baselines on the table: three working definitions of “available” · customer-facing incidents allowed: 0 · Blocker + Critical open = 0 at every go/no-go · four epic modules (reserve module, AFS engine, SNB, control screens) · per-environment workbooks Dev 310 / QA 255 / Perf 200 / Prod 221 rows · issues log 836 rows by program end.

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery (3/29 → 4/30) — profiled both books; built the bucket inventory with one proposed owning system per bucket; built the event inventory — POS flash sale, omni pending, ship-to-home confirm, S/4 Aggregation# posting, and MSEG movement types 101/311/601/701/702 as events. Exit bar: every bucket owned by exactly one system; every event named; sessions closed 4/30.
  • Plan & Design (5/15 → 8/14) — wrote the exactly-one-bucket rule, the reversal-references-original rule and the idempotency key into the FSD; kept Merch Reserve and Lost/Found outside S4_Avail so no double-subtract path exists; drafted the test-matrix skeleton. Exit bar: FSD signed 8/14 (record) with every bucket owned by exactly one system (the master’s discovery exit bar).
  • Build & Test (Aug → Jan/Apr) — authored each cell with before/after assertions; slotted epics per module; enforced “no story enters QA without a matrix cell reference”; closed SIT contract defects before UAT; ran the dated E2E days — Inbound 11/22, Dotcom 12/20, Retail 1/30. Exit bar: 0 open SIT contract defects; E2E green on three dates; Blocker + Critical open = 0.
  • Cutover & Go-Live (January; May 1 → Jul 1) — wrote the go/no-go recommendation from counted rows; the publish switch (OMS reads published AFS, never computes); reserve-copy invariants checked per SKU. Exit bar: no SKU with two published AFS values; Negative AFS clean at T+1; rollback never fired.
  • Hypercare / Close (Jun → Jul) — daily ISM-vs-MB52 drift; matrix grew from what the floor taught — four in-transit regression cells and the de-aggregation family (n lines · missing line · duplicate header) per event source. Exit bar: drift = 0 SKUs on consecutive days; matrix and control-owner map handed to the monthly release train.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the bucket inventory, the event inventory, the exactly-one-bucket rule, every cell’s assertions, the entry gate and the go/no-go recommendation. Built by others: BlueYonder, ISM and platform teams; QA ran the cells and owned the gate; DC Ops and planners confirmed in UAT because QA had already demoed each matrix family to them during build.

Designed the process; others own it now. The matrix, the entry gate and the regression families are standard work now: QA owns the gate, the control owners own the screens, and every later rule change enters as rows. Trade-off put to leadership: the date versus the bar — the gate was Blocker + Critical open = 0 and a countable cell list, not a feeling, so the go/no-go was arithmetic.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality
  • $ — no record dollar. The what-if (modeled, illustrative): 30,000 oversell-exposed incidents/yr ÷ 61 live cells = 491.8 per cell × $30 = ≈$14.8K/yr per escaped cell-class against ≈$26.8K one-time to author and run the matrix — break-even ≈1.8 escaped cell-classes. Not claimed as a saving.
  • # — 836 rows in the issues log, 164 Blocker + 277 Critical = 441 (52.8%), 714 closed (85.4%) · 4 in-transit regression cells + a 3-case de-aggregation family per event source · E2E days 11/22 · 12/20 · 1/30 (record).
  • % — Blocker + Critical open = 0 at each go/no-go; daily drift 0 SKUs on consecutive days at hypercare exit (record).
  • Time — FSD signed 8/14 · three dated E2E days · January and May go-lives · hypercare exit week 3 of 4 (record).
  • Customer — zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives; no SKU with two published AFS values (record).
  • Quality — every live cell asserted before/after; every event maps to exactly one bucket; a replayed event is a no-op (record design).

Probing — five facets, then the hardest follow-upsNEW
The five facets — where each one lives in this story
1 · Data analysis. Grain: SKU × node × channel × bucket × event. Sources: the bucket inventory (both books), the event inventory (POS, OMS, WMS, S/4 movement types 101/311/601/701/702), the 836-row issues log (severity, owner, function). Cuts: events by bucket cardinality (0 / 1 / 2), defects by severity and by seam. Revealed: the log shows where the book is heavy — 523 of 836 rows (62.6%) sit in the Outbound, Retail and Inbound flows, where an event crosses from one system to the next — but it carries no cell tag, so the matrix’s yield against it is not measured.
2 · Solution design. Options: (A) happy-path E2E only; (B) per-system unit tests; (C) an event × node-channel matrix with before/after assertions. Chose C — A and B each pass while the seam between systems is wrong. Why: the oversell bug is a seam bug, and a matrix makes coverage countable and gateable.
3 · Math & data logic. AFS = Basis − PickRes − EcomPick − EcomPack − SNB; Basis = min(S4_Avail, WMS_OnHand); cardinality rule: 0 buckets → +3 error (560), 2 buckets → −3 error (554), exactly 1 → 557; replay no-op; drift = ISM.S4_Avail − MB52 = 0; de-aggregation Σ per-line COGS = posting COGS to the cent.
4 · Implementation. Phases and gates above; owners: me (rule, cells, go/no-go), QA (gate), DC Ops/planners (UAT), control owners (screens). Controls: entry gate, E2E days, Blocker + Critical = 0, drift = 0. Risks: vocabulary drift, feed latency, override abuse (the register’s top three).
5 · Probe questions. Below: why a matrix, how you know it mattered, what it missed, what 61 cells cost, and the three trap drills.
Facet 5 — probe questions (challenge Q&A tuned to this interviewer)

Infra — why a matrix and not just more E2E days?

E2E proves the paths someone thought to walk; a matrix lists the cells nobody walked. We ran both — three dated E2E days — but the entry gate (no story enters QA without a cell reference) is what made coverage countable. The log tells you what broke; the matrix tells you what you never tried.

Infra — how do you know the matrix is what kept incidents at zero?

I can’t isolate it and I won’t claim to. The record is: zero customer-facing incidents, 441 Blocker/Critical rows dispositioned, Blocker + Critical open = 0 at each go/no-go. The number that would change my mind is a customer-facing incident traced to an event that had no cell.

Infra — what did the matrix miss?

My own first in-transit rule. The cells asserted the rule I wrote — “never promise in-transit” — and it passed every cell while starving dotcom of fast movers one day from the building. The fix added four cells and a back-test requirement: a matrix asserts the rule; only history tells you the rule is right.

Infra — what is a cell, concretely?

One event at one node-channel pair with a before state, an after state and the numbers: e.g. a store rings 3 units at 10:02 — AFS 560 → 557, SNB 40 → 43, replay at 10:04 → 557, overnight posting → S4_Avail 957, drift 0.

Infra — your 61-cell count — real?

No. Six node-channel pairs, 14 events, 84 candidate cells minus 36 structurally impossible = 48, plus 4 in-transit cells and a 3-case family on 3 event sources = 61 — all illustrative sizing. The record is the rule, the families and the log; I would confirm the actual count before quoting one.

Infra — what would you do differently?

Tag every issues-log row with its matrix cell from day one. I can say every story needed a cell reference; I cannot say what share of the 441 Blocker/Critical rows was found by a cell assertion rather than an E2E room. That is the one number that would prove the matrix’s yield.

Spoken statement

“What I can prove is the rule and the gate: every physical event maps to exactly one bucket, and no story entered QA without a cell reference. Through both go-lives there were zero customer-facing incidents, with 836 issues logged — 441 Blocker or Critical, 52.8% — and 714 closed. What I cannot prove is how many of those the matrix caught versus the E2E rooms. The dollar view is a what-if on illustrative inputs: if the matrix keeps about two cell-classes a year from escaping, it has paid for itself.”

If challenged

“Your cell count is invented.”

The design counts are illustrative sizing and I label them that way; the rule, the three de-aggregation cases, the four in-transit cells and the log are record. I would pull the real matrix from the QA workbooks before quoting a count.

“A matrix is shelfware — how do you know it was used?”

The entry gate: a story could not enter QA without a cell reference, and QA owned the gate and could fail a build. Use is enforced by the workflow, not by goodwill.
