Behavioral pack — four separate 90-second STAR answers, each from a different moment of the storyNEW
Dive Deep · AIQB Q1Q1. “Tell me about a time when you were trying to understand a complex problem on your team and you had to dig into the details to figure it out.”

I stopped reading oversell reports and started reading event traces — one SKU, one day, every event, every bucket. The pattern was never “the system is wrong”; it was an event landing in zero buckets or two. Take the worked SKU: 560 available. A store rings three units. If the event hits no bucket, AFS stays 560 and we promise three units that are gone. If it hits two — sold and shipped-not-billed both decrement — AFS goes to 554 and we hide three. Same event, three possible answers, one right: 557. So I wrote one rule — every event maps to exactly one bucket, a reversal must reference its original, a replay is a no-op — and turned it into before/after assertions per cell. Then I read the issues log against it: 836 rows, 441 Blocker or Critical. The defects were not random; they clustered where two systems used different grain. The result: zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives, and a rule the control owners could keep using after I left.

Number to land: 557 — not 560, not 554  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF the same trace is unload → scan → stow → sellable; I would pull one ASIN’s inbound event trace and look for events that land in zero or two states.  ·  Do NOT say: that I wrote the code or personally found the bugs; that the matrix alone kept incidents at zero.
Invent and Simplify · AIQB Q1Q2. “Give me an example of a complex problem you solved with a simple solution.”

About a dozen impacted systems, about thirty build tracks — and I refused to write thirty test plans. I wrote one asset: a matrix of physical events against node-and-channel cells, each with a before and after assertion, and one entry rule — no story enters QA without a cell reference. Epics were per module — reserve module, AFS engine, SNB, control screens — slotted by business criticality × effort, so every track mapped onto the same cells. That turned “is it ready?” from an argument into a count: Blocker plus Critical open equals zero, and every live cell green. When my first in-transit rule turned out wrong, the matrix absorbed the fix as four new rows, not a new project. The same logic made a new node or channel a matrix row. The result was zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives with 85.4% of 836 logged issues closed.

Number to land: one rule · one asset · one gate (cell reference to enter QA)  ·  Pivot to NACF: NACF launches many parallel tracks — a new vendor cohort, a new IXD lane; one event × node matrix gives a countable ready-to-launch gate.  ·  Do NOT say: the 61-cell count as fact; that I built the tooling.
Insist on the Highest Standards · AIQB Q2Q3. “Tell me about a time when you were unsatisfied with the status quo. What did you do to change it?”

I changed what “tested” meant: not ‘we ran the flow’ but ‘every live cell asserted, with numbers, before and after.’ Each cell carried an expected state — AFS 560 to 557, SNB 40 to 43, drift zero — so a pass was a number, not an opinion. Then I put the standard in rooms, not memos: three dated end-to-end days — Inbound 11/22, Dotcom 12/20, Retail 1/30 — with every function in one room, every finding logged with a severity and an owner before anyone left. The go/no-go bar was written in advance: Blocker plus Critical open equals zero, mock three inside the 0.5% tolerance, runbook rehearsed, rollback armed. I said I would have called no-go on one open Blocker — and I never had to. The result: zero customer-facing incidents, and a gate QA, not I, defended.

Number to land: three dated E2E days; Blocker + Critical open = 0  ·  Pivot to NACF: Before any NACF launch I would publish the same bar — what the gate asserts, in numbers, and who may fail it.  ·  Do NOT say: that I never faced pressure; that the bar was only mine — QA owned the gate.
Earn Trust · AIQB Q5Q4. “Tell me about a time when your team’s goals were out of alignment with another team you relied on in order to meet your goal.”

DC Ops did not trust a systems program to respect the floor, so I made sure nothing reached them as a surprise. During build, QA demoed each matrix family — receipts, transfers, pick and pack reserves, ship confirms — to DC Ops and the planners. By the time UAT started, their sign-off was confirmation, not discovery. QA owned the gate and could fail a build, and DC Ops saw that it could. I also kept the floor’s reality inside the design: the January planning go-live (purchasing and inbound, reserve rules live against demand) left the floor untouched, and warehouse execution moved only in May. The result was go-lives where DC Ops owned the robotics gate and the dial and the floor ran on them, with zero customer-facing incidents.

Number to land: January reserve rules first; May execution second; zero incidents  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF I would demo each inbound event family to the building before the first live trailer — the same ‘confirmation, not discovery’ rule.  ·  Do NOT say: that I ran the floor; DC Ops did.
Same story, three angles — data · conflict/influence/backbone · failure-learningNEW
Data angle · Dive Deep“Start with the log: 836 rows, 441 of them Blocker or Critical — 52.8%.”
Headline metric: 441 / 836 = 52.8% high-severity; 714 closed = 85.4%
Cut the log by severity, owner and seam; the matrix made coverage countable. What it revealed: 62.6% of the rows sit in the three flows where an event crosses systems (Outbound 238, Retail 172, Inbound 113 = 523, computed from the file), which is where the matrix cells concentrate. Honest limit: rows were not tagged by cell.		Conflict / influence angle · Earn Trust“I owned a test asset for ~30 build tracks in other orgs and none of them reported to me.”
Headline metric: ~30 build tracks · one entry gate
The gate (no story enters QA without a cell reference) moved every track onto one asset; QA owned it and could fail a build; DC Ops confirmed because QA had already demoed to them. Influence came from a countable rule, not rank.		Failure / learning angle · Are Right, A Lot“My own first in-transit rule passed every cell I wrote — and it was wrong.”
Headline metric: σ ≈ 0.3 d → window ≈ 0.49 d ≈ 95%; 4 cells added
“Never promise in-transit” starved dotcom of fast movers one day from the building; the matrix asserted my rule, not whether the rule was right. Now: both sides of a rule get a number, back-test against history first. (Record: master S4.)
Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups, with numeric answersNEW
How do you know it was the matrix and not the E2E days or the team?

You cannot, and I would not claim it. Record: zero customer-facing incidents, 441 Blocker/Critical rows dispositioned, B+C open = 0 at go/no-go. The measurable yield is the share of the 441 first detected by a cell assertion — the log was not tagged by cell, so I cannot produce it.

Say: “I can’t isolate the matrix from the E2E days; the number that would change my mind is a customer-facing incident traced to an event with no cell.”

Why is a 61-cell matrix not over-engineering against an 836-row log?

The log is the detector; the matrix is the coverage map. At 4 h a cell, 61 cells is 244 h ≈ $26.8K (modeled); one escaped cell-class is ≈$14.8K/yr on illustrative inputs — break-even ≈1.8 cell-classes.

Say: “The log tells me what broke; the matrix tells me what I never tried — and it costs less than two escaped cell-classes.”

What did the matrix not catch, and what changed?

The v1 in-transit rule: cells asserted my rule and it was wrong; the fix added four cells and a back-test-before-publish requirement (σ ≈ 0.3 d, window ≈ 0.49 d ≈ 95%).

Say: “A matrix checks that the system does what the rule says; only history tells you whether the rule is right — so now both sides of a rule get a number first.”

20-second version + NACF bridgeNEW

20-second version. At the Utah DC I made “tested” countable: every physical event had to land in exactly one inventory bucket, each cell asserted before and after, and no story entered QA without a cell reference — zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives, with 441 of 836 logged issues Blocker or Critical and none reaching a customer.

NACF bridge. NACF inbound has the same seam — a trailer unloaded, a pallet received, a unit stowed, a unit sellable. The booklet’s “stranded” inventory is exactly an event that landed in zero states. I would build the same event × node matrix for receive → stow → sellable so every dock event lands in exactly one inventory state before a new FC ramps.

U2 — The profile before the design — SKU overlap, order shape and demand variability per node across both books (MCS and S/4): the discovery that sized the one-pool case and bounded its $2.2MNEW

LPs this story serves here: Dive Deep (primary — both books at SKU × node grain) · Are Right, A Lot (a bounded claim with a kill number) · Learn and Be Curious (backup — the pooling math and the retail/dotcom order shapes) · Think Big (backup — two pools to one)
Process family: Plan / Source — inventory positioning and pooling analysis; the discovery profile that sized the case for one comingle pool
Honesty label. Record: Discovery (3/29 → 4/30) profiled both books — MCS and S/4 — and the 801/901 nodes for SKU overlap and order profiles per node, with node-by-node sessions across ~15 functions; the design argument started from their data. The √2 pooling release, the $50M average inventory × 25% safety stock × 60% overlap and the ≈$1.6M/yr are S2's modeled numbers on an illustrative 100K-units/week DC. Not in the record: the profile’s actual outputs — measured overlap, σ ratio, correlation, order-shape cuts. Every range below is toolkit math on labeled inputs. Never say the profile “measured” a release; say it bounded one.
Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each
Dive Deep. I profiled both books at SKU × node grain before any design argument, so the debate started from their own data instead of opinions about it.
Are Right, A Lot. I refused to carry the textbook $2.2M as a point: I showed what would shrink it (unequal pools, correlation), bounded it at ≈$0.74M–$2.20M, and named the measurement that settles it.
Learn and Be Curious. I worked the variance-pooling formulas by hand on small numbers and learned the retail-vs-dotcom order shapes in the node sessions, so I could ask the right three questions of the data.
Think Big. The recommendation was structural — two pools to one, with three planning identities — and it stood even when the pooling dollars shrank, because the case never rested on them alone.
60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Before anyone argued design at Sephora’s Utah DC, there were two inventory books — MCS and S/4 — and two systemic warehouses in one building, Dotcom 801 and Retail 901, and a business case that said “pool them.” Whether pooling was worth it turned on three things nobody had put side by side: how many SKUs both pools carried, whether the two nodes’ orders looked alike, and how variable and how correlated the demand was. Task. Own the discovery profile that the location and inventory model would stand on — about a dozen impacted systems, 60+ stakeholders, none reporting to me. Action. Discovery ran 3/29 to 4/30: both books profiled for SKU overlap and order profiles per node, node-by-node sessions across about fifteen functions. I turned the profile into three tests — overlap, order shape, variability — and a bounded claim: textbook √2 pooling on $7.5M of duplicated poolable safety stock is a $2.2M ceiling; unequal or correlated pools cut it to roughly $0.7M to $2.2M, and about 87% of the $1.6M a year modeled benefit does not depend on pooling at all. That fed the decision — one physical pool, three planning identities. Result. Primary metric: the one-time pooling claim moved from a single textbook number to a bounded range, about $0.7M to $2.2M, modeled, with discovery closed on 4/30. The +15% and the zero incidents are S2’s record; the profile outputs are illustrative and I would not quote them as measured.

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — Two systemic warehouses in one building transferring stock to itself, two books (MCS and S/4), a case for one comingle pool resting on three unmeasured assumptions: that both pools carry the same SKUs, that their orders look alike enough to share a pick face, and that pooling their demand variability would release stock. A pooling claim built on a textbook assumption is the first number a skeptic pulls on.

T — Produce the profile the design could stand on and a claim that survives the first pull: which SKUs overlap, what each node’s order looks like, how much of the $2.2M survives unequal and correlated pools — and which measurement turns the model into a record. None of the systems or analysts reported to me; ~15 functions sat in the sessions.

Baselines on the table: planning pools 2 · average inventory $50M (illustrative) × 25% safety stock × 60% held in SKUs both pools carry = $7.5M duplicated poolable stock · self-transfers ≈260,000 units/yr ≈5.0% of an illustrative 5.2M units/yr · efficiency index 100.

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery (3/29 → 4/30) — pulled both books at SKU × node grain; cut for overlap (which SKUs live in both 801 and 901), order shape per node (retail store-replenishment style vs dotcom small-order style), and demand variability per SKU; ran node-by-node sessions with ~15 functions; wrote the bucket inventory. Exit bar: sessions closed 4/30; every bucket owned by one system; open questions carried with owner and date.
  • Plan & Design (5/15 → 8/14) — turned the profile into the three-identity design (one physical location, three planning lenses) and into a bounded pooling claim in the sponsor pack: ceiling $2.2M, plausible floor ≈$0.74M, recurring benefit mostly independent of pooling. Exit bar: council sign-off 7/31 and FSD 8/14 with the range, not a point, in the case.
  • Build & Test (Aug → Jan/Apr) — carried the profile’s cuts into the matrix rows (U1) and the interim shadow monitor; the node/channel/order-shape cuts became test families. Exit bar: matrix families trace to a profile cut; E2E days 11/22 · 12/20 · 1/30.
  • Cutover & Go-Live (January; May 1 → Jul 1) — no new analysis on the critical path — the profile’s job was done at design; it re-entered only as the reference for the “which book does this report read” declaration during the interim. Exit bar: every report declared its system of record for the interim.
  • Hypercare / Close (Jun → Jul) — the record’s own firming measurement — SKU-level on-hand on the last day of 801 against the first stable month of 901 — is the way the release becomes a record; flagged as the open measurement. Exit bar: pooling stays labeled “modeled” until that comparison is run.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the profile design, the three tests, the bounded claim, the design recommendation. Others’: the extracts (platform/data teams), the node sessions (DC Ops, Planning, Digital, Finance), the sign-off (steering). Digital’s worry that a virtual Retail DC would blur their forecasting lens was answered by the design — three planning identities over one physical location — not by an argument.

Designed the process; others own it now. The profile cuts (overlap · order shape · variability) are the template the next DC’s discovery reused; the three-identity design is the standing location model. Trade-off put to leadership: a precise-looking $2.2M versus an honest range — I took the range, because the first number a skeptic pulls on is the one resting on an assumption.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality
  • $ — pooling release (one-time, modeled): textbook ceiling $7.5M × 29.3% = ≈$2.2M; plausible floor ≈$0.74M at σ ratio 1/3 and ρ = 0.5. Recurring ≈$1.43M (87% of ≈$1.65M) does not depend on pooling. All modeled on S2’s illustrative DC — never signed.
  • # — 2 books profiled · 2 planning pools → 1 · 3 planning identities · self-transfers ≈260,000 units/yr (≈5.0% of illustrative volume) → 0 by construction (record design).
  • % — release range 9.9%–29.3% of poolable stock; overlap sensitivity 40/60/80% → $1.46M / $2.20M / $2.93M (modeled).
  • Time — sessions closed 4/30 (record); the profile preceded council 5/15 → sign-off 7/31 → FSD 8/14.
  • Customer — zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives (S2 record) — the constraint the one-pool design had to live inside.
  • Quality — every pooling dollar carries its input and basis label; the claim names the measurement that would firm it.

Probing — five facets, then the hardest follow-upsNEW
The five facets — where each one lives in this story
1 · Data analysis. Grain: SKU × node (801 / 901 / stores) in both books. Joins: MCS ↔ S/4 on SKU; overlap flag by node pair; demand history per SKU per node. Cuts: overlap by value; order shape per node (lines per order, units per line — illustrative until pulled); σ and correlation per overlapping SKU. Revealed (modeled): the case is most sensitive to correlation and σ ratio, and least to overlap, once the textbook is replaced by the range.
2 · Solution design. Options: (A) keep two pools and reconcile (status quo); (B) pool physically but plan as one blended identity; (C) one physical pool, three planning identities. Chose C — A keeps self-transfers; B blurs the forecasting lens Digital needed. Why: the profile showed the case did not need the safety-stock row to be big.
3 · Math & data logic. Equal independent pools: release = 1 − 1/√n = 1 − 1/√2 = 29.3%. Unequal: 1 − √(σ₁²+σ₂²)/(σ₁+σ₂). Correlated: 1 − √(σ₁²+σ₂²+2ρσ₁σ₂)/(σ₁+σ₂). Dollars = poolable stock × release; carry = release × 10%. Break-even sentence: the claim fails only if the recurring rows fail.
4 · Implementation. Discovery 3/29 → 4/30; design to FSD 8/14; the profile cuts carried into matrix rows; the SKU-level on-hand comparison (last day 801 vs first stable month 901) is the firming measurement. Owners: me (profile and claim), data teams (extracts), Finance (inventory valuation).
5 · Probe questions. Below: why not trust √2, how you know the 60%, what if overlap is 20%, did the profile change the design, and the three trap drills.
Facet 5 — probe questions (challenge Q&A tuned to this interviewer)

Infra — why not just trust √2?

Because it assumes two equal, independent pools on the same SKUs — three assumptions at once. I showed the release at σ ratio 1, 1/2, 1/3 and 1/4 (29.3%, 25.5%, 20.9%, 17.5%) and at correlation 0.3 and 0.5 (19.4%, 13.4%); jointly pessimistic it is 9.9%. The textbook is the ceiling, not the base.

Infra — how do you know the overlap is 60%?

I don’t — it is S2’s illustrative input, the share of poolable stock in SKUs both pools carry. The profile is what measures it; at 20% overlap the equal-pool release is $0.73M, at 80% $2.93M. The case never depended on getting that one right.

Infra — if the release is that uncertain, why pool at all?

Because the pooling carry is only 13% of the modeled recurring benefit. Labor at +15% (≈$0.88M, record efficiency), split shipments (≈$0.45M) and self-transfer handling (≈$0.10M) are $1.43M; self-transfers alone were ≈260,000 units a year of movement that created no value.

Infra — did the profile change the design?

It shaped it: it is why I kept three planning identities over one physical location rather than one blended lens, and why the matrix has rows per node type and order shape. I would not claim it changed the decision to pool; that case was already strong on the recurring rows.

Infra — what would you pull first at a new node?

SKU-level on-hand and sales for the two pools, joined by SKU: overlap by value, σ and correlation per SKU, then order shape — lines per order and units per line. Three pulls, one afternoon, and the claim has inputs.

Spoken statement

“The profile did two things. It told me which SKUs, which node shapes and how much variability the case rested on, and it kept me from carrying a textbook number as a fact. The pooling release is about $2.2 million at the ceiling — equal, independent pools — and about three-quarters of a million at a pessimistic corner; I show the range. What does not move is that about 87% of the modeled benefit is labor at the signed +15%, split shipments and self-transfers, none of which depend on pooling. The thing that turns the model into a record is one comparison: SKU-level on-hand the last day of 801 against the first stable month of 901.”

If challenged

“So the $2.2M in S2 is wrong?”

It is the ceiling on S2’s stated inputs, and S2 labels it modeled and textbook. This story is the bounding of it — what survives if the pools are unequal or correlated — not a retraction.

“You never ran the comparison?”

I would not claim it. The comparison is the measurement the record proposes; until it is run the number stays labeled modeled. If you ask me whether pooling released that stock, my answer is: here is the range and here is the one-afternoon pull that settles it.

Behavioral pack — four separate 90-second STAR answers, each from a different moment of the storyNEW
Dive Deep · AIQB Q3Q1. “Tell me about a problem you had to solve that required in-depth thought and analysis.”

The accepted assumption was that two pools become one and safety stock drops by 29%. That is the square-root pooling result, and it assumes the two pools are equal and independent — which retail and dotcom demand for the same SKU almost certainly are not. So in discovery I stopped quoting the textbook and asked the data three questions: which SKUs do both pools carry, how big is each pool’s variability relative to the other, and how correlated are they? I put the answer as a range instead of a point. Equal and independent gives 29.3%. If one pool is a third the size of the other, 20.9%. If correlation is 0.5, 13.4%. Jointly pessimistic, 9.9%. On $7.5 million of duplicated poolable stock that is $0.74 million to $2.2 million. Then I checked whether the case still stood: about 87% of the modeled benefit — labor at the signed +15%, split shipments, self-transfers — does not depend on pooling at all. The design stood; the claim became honest.

Number to land: 9.9%–29.3% → ≈$0.74M–$2.2M; 87% independent of pooling  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF the same three pulls decide whether two FCs’ buffers belong together — same ASINs, same demand shape, correlation — before I claim any release.  ·  Do NOT say: that the profile measured the release; that $2.2M is a record.
Are Right, A Lot · AIQB Q1Q2. “Tell me about a time when you didn't have enough data to make the right decision.”

I made the recommendation robust to the thing I did not know. I did not have measured σ or correlation for the overlapping SKUs when the sponsor pack was written. So I built the claim as a range with a kill number: the only way the one-pool recommendation fails is if the recurring rows fail, and those rest on a signed +15% efficiency, a split-shipment row I flagged as the softest, and the self-transfer move that disappears by construction. Then I named the measurement that converts model to record — SKU-level on-hand on the last day of 801 against the first stable month of 901 — and I told steering what number would change my mind: if split shipments didn’t move, the total drops to about $1.1 million and I would say so. The recommendation was the same across the whole range, which is the test of a decision made with incomplete data.

Number to land: recommendation stable from $1.50M to $1.65M recurring  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF placement decisions are made on incomplete data constantly; the habit I would bring is a range, a kill number and the one measurement that firms it.  ·  Do NOT say: that I knew the overlap; that the model was validated.
Learn and Be Curious · AIQB Q1Q3. “Tell me about a time when you realized you needed a deeper level of subject matter expertise to do your job well.”

I started by asking what the word “pooling” actually assumes. The business case said “pooling,” and I wanted to know exactly what that word assumed. So I worked the variance-pooling toolkit — equal independent pools, unequal pools, correlation — and checked each formula by hand on small numbers until I could do it on a whiteboard. Then I learned the shapes of the two nodes’ orders: retail store-replenishment style versus dotcom single-customer orders, because that is what decides whether two pools can share a pick face. I sat in the node sessions with about fifteen functions asking what a normal day looked like. By the end of discovery I could explain, in one page, why the pooling number was a ceiling and what each node’s order looked like. Zero customer-facing incidents later is not mine alone, but the design started from a profile I could defend.

Number to land: three formulas hand-checked; ~15 functions in node sessions  ·  Pivot to NACF: NACF placement is the same toolkit — I would arrive having learned the ASIN-level demand shapes before proposing a buffer.  ·  Do NOT say: that I invented the pooling math; it is the toolkit’s, and I label it.
Think Big · AIQB Q1Q4. “Tell me about time when you were working on an initiative or goal and saw an opportunity to do something much bigger or better than the initial focus.”

The ask was to merge two warehouses’ reporting; the bigger structure was one physical pool. Two systemic warehouses sat in one building and moved stock to each other — about 260,000 units a year on paper, roughly 5% of volume that created no value. I proposed collapsing them into one pool with three planning identities so each channel kept its forecasting lens without a second truth. Digital worried a virtual retail DC would blur their lens; I made the concern the design — the three identities — rather than arguing. The bigger structure was riskier, so I staged it: two go-lives, each carrying one defect class. And I made sure the case did not hang on the most uncertain number: even at the pessimistic pooling corner, the recurring benefit is about $1.5 million. Big, but with the downside bounded.

Number to land: ≈260K units/yr self-transfers → 0; 2 pools → 1  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF a bigger structure — fewer buffers pooled upstream — needs the same discipline: bounded downside before ambition.  ·  Do NOT say: that pooling alone paid for it; that the dollars are signed.
Same story, three angles — data · conflict/influence/backbone · failure-learningNEW
Data angle · Dive Deep“The textbook says 29%. Here is what survives when the pools are unequal and correlated.”
Headline metric: 9.9%–29.3% release; $0.74M–$2.20M
Three tests (overlap, σ ratio, correlation) turned one number into a surface; the case’s dependence on pooling is ≤13%. Honest limit: the inputs are illustrative until the profile is pulled.		Conflict / influence angle · Earn Trust“Digital worried a virtual Retail DC would blur their forecasting lens — the profile gave me a way to say yes to them.”
Headline metric: 3 planning identities over 1 physical location
The concern became the design: each channel kept its lens; no second truth. No one had to lose an argument. Steering signed the criteria; DC Ops and Planning confirmed the node sessions.		Failure / learning angle · Are Right, A Lot“In S2 I say $2.2M one-time — that is the equal-pools ceiling, and I should lead with the range.”
Headline metric: $2.2M point → $0.74M–$2.20M range
I label it modeled and textbook, but a point invites the first pull and the first pull finds the equal-independent assumption. Do differently: say the range first, name the kill number, and never let the ceiling stand alone. confirm whether the sponsor pack carried the point or the range
Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups, with numeric answersNEW
What number would have changed your mind about pooling?

If the recurring rows failed: the split-shipment row (≈$0.45M, softest) not moving drops the total to ≈$1.1M; if the +15% were not signed the case would be different. Pooling’s carry is only $0.07M–$0.22M/yr, so no outcome of the pooling math alone could flip it.

Say: “The pooling row is at most 13% of the benefit, so the number that would change my mind is split shipments or the signed efficiency — not the safety-stock release.”

Isn’t the 60% overlap just made up?

It is S2’s illustrative input — said so. At 20% overlap the equal-pool release is $0.73M; at 80% it is $2.93M. The profile’s job is to replace it with the measured value, join by SKU, value-weighted.

Say: “It is an illustrative input I label as such; here is the one-afternoon pull that replaces it, and here is what the answer does to the claim across 20% to 80%.”

How do you know it was pooling and not seasonality that moved stock?

I can’t — and I don’t claim it. The record has +15% efficiency measured by DC Ops post-stabilization and zero incidents; the pooling release is modeled. The firming comparison is last-day 801 versus first stable month of 901 on SKU-level on-hand, seasonally matched.

Say: “The pooling dollars are modeled; if you want it as a record, it is a seasonally-matched SKU-level on-hand comparison — that is the one measurement I would run.”

20-second version + NACF bridgeNEW

20-second version. Before the design argument at Sephora’s Utah DC I profiled both inventory books for SKU overlap and order shape per node and turned the textbook pooling number — $2.2M — into a bounded range of about $0.74M to $2.2M, with about 87% of the modeled benefit not depending on pooling at all; that range, not a point, went into the sponsor pack.

NACF bridge. NACF’s placement and buffer questions start with the same three pulls — do two FCs carry the same ASINs, do they see the same demand shape, how correlated are they — because pooling variability upstream only pays where correlation is low, and an INV-D transfer is how the pooled unit actually moves. I would bring the range-and-kill-number habit to any buffer-positioning claim.

U3 — Merch reserve — visible, never promisable: pricing the no (and the asymmetry behind it) so one comingled pool never sells stock the floor cannot pickNEW

LPs this story serves here: Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit (primary — the priced no) · Customer Obsession (oversell is a customer defect) · Earn Trust (visibility offered, the no written as a row) · Success and Scale Bring Broad Responsibility (a customer harm, not a ledger error)
Process family: Plan / Deliver — comingled-inventory allocation at a shared node: which stock is promisable to which channel, and which is physically present but not for sale
Honesty label. Record: Merch Reserve and Lost/Found sit outside S4_Avail, so there is no double-subtract path; Merchandising wanted merch reserve promisable; I priced it (unpickable stock sold) and offered visibility without promisability; the no went into the FSD as a row, not a meeting minute; the asymmetry (oversell is a customer defect, undersell a planning variance) is the policy; comingle-reserve improvements were held out of the release to protect zero incidents and priced as a later enhancement at steering; zero customer-facing oversell incidents through both go-lives. Illustrative: the 20% release rate, the 10,000-unit reserve, the 50-unit worked SKU and the retail/dotcom pool split. The margin $19 and the $6 + $4 + $1 + $19 = $30 incident cost are S1’s benchmark. I do not know how Merchandising defined or sized the reserve. I owned the definition and the no; Merchandising owned the reserve.
Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each
Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit. Merchandising wanted merch reserve promisable; I priced it, said no, and wrote the no into the FSD as a row so it survived the room.
Customer Obsession. I treated oversell as a customer defect and undersell as a planning variance — and accepted visible undersell to make oversell zero by construction.
Earn Trust. I gave Merchandising what they actually needed — visibility of the reserve — so the no cost them a lever, not their standing.
Success and Scale Bring Broad Responsibility. A promise on stock the floor cannot pick is a customer harm at scale; the price in dollars was the floor of the argument, not the whole of it.
60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. At Sephora’s Utah DC one physical pool had to serve two channels, and Merchandising held some of that stock back as reserve. Merchandising wanted the reserve promisable — it was in the building, so why not sell it. Task. I owned the definition of “available” across Store, Retail DC and Dotcom DC, and I owned no system. Action. Discovery: a bucket inventory where every bucket had one owning system; merch reserve and lost/found sat outside the supply the AFS engine computes from, so there was no double-subtract path. Plan & Design: I priced the ask — stock the floor may not pick, sold — as a break-even: promise it only if more than roughly 37 of every 100 reserved units would actually be released, and on a 20% release rate that is about −$50,000 per 10,000 units, or about −$200,000 on the all-in cost. Then I offered what Merchandising actually needed: visibility without promisability. The no went into the FSD as a row, signed by Strategy, Inventory Management and Inventory Accounting. Comingle-reserve improvements were held out of the release to protect zero incidents and priced as a later enhancement. Result. Primary metric: customer-facing oversell incidents — recurring across three node types → zero through both go-lives, with zero units of merch reserve in published AFS. The dollars are a what-if on labeled inputs; the zero is the record.

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — One physical pool, two channels, three planning identities — and stock physically in the building that was not for sale: Merchandising’s reserve, lost-and-found. Merchandising wanted the reserve promisable; Finance wanted on-hand to tie to the book; dotcom wanted in-transit promisable. Most oversell bugs are vocabulary bugs, and “available” had three meanings.

T — Define, per node and per channel, which buckets are subtracted from supply and which sit outside it — and say no to the one request that would have turned unpickable stock into a promise. I owned no system; the signatories were Supply Chain Strategy, Inventory Management and FSC Inventory Accounting.

Baselines on the table: working definitions of “available” 3 · customer-facing oversell incidents recurring across three node types, target zero · worked SKU: Basis 980, buckets 420, AFS 560 · merch reserve and lost/found outside S4_Avail.

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery (3/29 → 4/30) — bucket inventory with one proposed owning system per bucket; node-by-node sessions; Merchandising’s need written down as a need — see the reserve — not as a mechanism. Exit bar: every bucket owned by exactly one system; sessions closed 4/30.
  • Plan & Design (5/15 → 8/14) — kept merch reserve and lost/found outside S4_Avail; wrote the lower-of-two basis and the AFS equation; priced the promisable-reserve ask as a break-even; offered visibility without promisability; held the asymmetry. Exit bar: FSD signed 8/14; the no is a row; every bucket owned by exactly one system.
  • Build & Test (Aug → Jan/Apr) — matrix cells that assert the reserve never appears in published AFS (U1) and a visibility view for Merchandising; held the comingle-reserve improvements out of the release. Exit bar: Blocker + Critical open = 0; reserve-copy invariants checked per SKU.
  • Cutover & Go-Live (January; May 1 → Jul 1) — reserve rules live against demand at the January planning go-live with the floor untouched; execution moved in May; no SKU with two published AFS values. Exit bar: oversell incidents zero through both go-lives.
  • Hypercare / Close (Jun → Jul) — daily ISM-vs-MB52 drift; Negative AFS and AFS Correction under control owners (U6); the improvement list handed to steering as a priced later enhancement. Exit bar: drift 0 on consecutive days; enhancement priced, not promised.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the bucket inventory, the break-even, the asymmetry, the offer of visibility, the row in the FSD. Merchandising owned the reserve; control owners own the screens. Digital’s forecasting-lens concern was answered by the three-identity design, not by a ruling.

Designed the process; others own it now. The reserve rule is now a row in the FSD and a cell family in the matrix; Merchandising sees the reserve and Inventory Control chases variance on the screens. Trade-off put to leadership: sell the reserve and accept unpickable-stock cancellations, or hold it back and accept visible undersell — I priced both and recommended undersell.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality
  • $ — no record dollar for this decision. What-if (modeled, illustrative): at a 20% release rate, promising 10,000 reserved units costs −$50,000 (incremental cancellation cost) to −$202,000 (S1’s all-in $30 per incident) against the ≈$38,000 of expected sales; break-even release 36.7% (61.2% all-in). Not claimed as a saving.
  • # — 3 working definitions → 1 signed AFS; 0 units of merch reserve in published AFS (record design); 3 organizations signed the row.
  • % — break-even release rate p* = 36.7% (modeled); oversell rate behind S1’s ≈$0.9M row is 0.10% of orders (benchmark 0.05–0.25%; the record is incidents → zero).
  • Time — FSD signed 8/14; January reserve go-live; May execution go-live (record).
  • Customer — customer-facing oversell incidents recurring → zero through two go-lives (record); no SKU with two published AFS values.
  • Quality — reserve and lost/found outside S4_Avail — no double-subtract path; the no lives in the FSD as a signed row (record design).

Probing — five facets, then the hardest follow-upsNEW
The five facets — where each one lives in this story
1 · Data analysis. Grain: SKU × node × channel × bucket. Sources: the bucket inventory, both books’ on-hand, the reserve view Merchandising worked from. Cuts: stock by bucket (inside supply vs outside), by channel demand, by pool. Revealed: stock present in the building and absent from S4_Avail is physical truth the system must not promise.
2 · Solution design. Options: (A) reserve promisable; (B) reserve visible, not promisable (chosen); (C) reserve invisible. A overpromises on a release the floor cannot guarantee; C creates shadow spreadsheets. B gives Merchandising the lever they needed.
3 · Math & data logic. AFS = Basis − PickRes − EcomPick − EcomPack − SNB; MR outside S4_Avail → AFS stays 560 whether MR = 0 or 50. Break-even p* = c ÷ (g + c) = 11 ÷ 30 = 36.7%; EV per unit = p × $19 − (1 − p) × $11.
4 · Implementation. FSD row signed 8/14; matrix cells; visibility view; January/May go-lives; control owners for the screens. Risks: override abuse, vocabulary drift, feed latency (the register’s top three).
5 · Probe questions. Below: how you priced it, what if the release is higher, who lost, what you deferred, and the three trap drills.
Facet 5 — probe questions (challenge Q&A tuned to this interviewer)

Infra — it was in the building. Why not sell it?

Physical presence is not pickability. The reserve is stock Merchandising controls; if it is promised and not released, the customer finds out. I priced it: at a 20% release rate, 10,000 units is −$50K on the cancellation cost alone, −$202K on S1’s all-in cost — and it is a customer harm beyond the dollars.

Infra — what release rate would make you say yes?

More than 36.7% on the incremental-cost view, 61.2% on the all-in view. At 40% the incremental EV is +$10K per 10,000 units — thin. I would also want a hold-until date and a release SLA — a rate alone is not a promise.

Infra — who lost?

Merchandising could not sell the reserve in the channel until it was released; they kept full visibility. The comingle-reserve improvements were held out of the release and priced as a later enhancement (master S1); the architecture-council pack had already fenced them out of scope in writing — ‘the comingle reserve math stays untouched’ (Apple C4).

Infra — how does one pool serve two channels without a split?

Both channels read the same Basis; every event decrements one bucket. Two-pool example: retail 300 with demand 250 (50 idle), dotcom 260 with demand 290 (30 short) — one pool of 560 meets 540 with 20 left. The cost is that the pool’s AFS must be right, which is the matrix’s job (U1).

Infra — what is the downside of this policy?

Visible undersell: the SKU shows less than the floor could sell. On S1’s benchmark undersell costs ≈$4.75 per unit against ≈$30 for an oversell — about 6.3 to 1 — and Inventory Control chases it on the screens. I accept that trade on purpose.

Spoken statement

“Merchandising asked me to make the reserve promisable. I said no, and I priced the no: promising stock that may not be released is worth it only above about thirty-seven percent release on the incremental cost, sixty-one on S1’s all-in cost; on a twenty percent release rate that is about fifty thousand dollars per ten thousand units on the low view and about two hundred thousand on the high view — illustrative inputs, and I show them. What I offered was visibility without promisability, and the no went into the FSD as a signed row. The record is zero customer-facing oversell incidents through both go-lives; the dollars are what that decision would have cost.”

If challenged

“Isn’t that just a made-up release rate?”

The 20% is illustrative and I tag it. What is not illustrative is the shape: the decision flips only above p* = 36.7% (61.2% all-in). The measurement I would ask Merchandising for is the historical release rate by SKU class.

“You priced undersell at $4.75 — where from?”

$19 margin × a 25% share of undersold demand that does not come back — both illustrative; it is a 1 : 6.3 asymmetry on S1’s benchmark, not a measured number. If the share were 100% the ratio would still be 1.6 : 1 against promising.

Behavioral pack — four separate 90-second STAR answers, each from a different moment of the storyNEW
Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit · AIQB Q1Q1. “Tell me about a time when you strongly disagreed with your manager or peer on something you considered very important to the business.”

Merchandising wanted the merch reserve to be promisable — stock in the building, why not sell it? I disagreed, but not by arguing. I priced the ask: promising a unit that may not be released only pays if more than about 37% of reserved units actually are released; on a 20% release rate that is about minus fifty thousand dollars per ten thousand units on the cancellation cost alone and about minus two hundred thousand on S1’s all-in cost. Then I offered what they actually needed: visibility — the reserve shown, not promised. The no went into the FSD as a row signed by Strategy, Inventory Management and Inventory Accounting, so it was a decision, not a meeting minute that would be re-litigated weekly. The comingle-reserve improvements were held out of the release to protect zero incidents and priced as a later enhancement at steering. I disagreed once, with a number, and committed in writing.

Number to land: p* = 36.7%; −$50K to −$202K per 10,000 units (illustrative)  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF the same break-even decides when to promise ahead of a physical state — received-not-stowed, in transit — a number, not a feeling.  ·  Do NOT say: that I won; that the dollars are saved; that the release rate was measured.
Customer Obsession · AIQB Q7Q2. “Sometimes customers make unreasonable requests. Tell me about a time when you've had to push back or say no to a customer request.”

I accepted visible undersell so that oversell would be zero by construction. The design policy was an asymmetry: an oversell is a customer defect — someone pays for something we do not have — and an undersell is a planning variance Inventory Control chases. On S1’s benchmark one oversell costs about thirty dollars and an undersold unit about five, roughly a six-to-one asymmetry, so the supply basis is the lower of the two books and the reserve never enters it. That means some sellable stock shows as unavailable. I said so in the FSD and made the control owners own the screens that chase it. The result: customer-facing oversell incidents that were recurring across three node types went to zero through both go-lives. The cost of the policy is visible undersell, which I will always take over a broken promise.

Number to land: oversell incidents recurring → zero; asymmetry ≈ 1 : 6.3  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF a promise date is a customer commitment; I would take a slightly conservative buffer over a missed date on the same arithmetic.  ·  Do NOT say: a customer count; that undersell is free.
Earn Trust · AIQB Q3Q3. “Describe a time when you needed to influence a peer who had a differing opinion about a shared goal.”

The relationship was the reason I started with what Merchandising needed, not what they asked for. They asked for the reserve to be promisable; what they needed was to see it and to know when it could be released. So the first thing I said was yes: yes to visibility, in ISM, alongside AFS. Then I said no to the mechanism and showed my arithmetic — the break-even and the cancellation cost — so the no was not an opinion. I made the cost to them explicit: the reserve could not be sold in the channel until released. I put the no into the FSD as a row so nobody had to defend it in a hallway, and the comingle-reserve improvements were held out of the release and priced rather than refused. The FSD was signed by three orgs (user anchor, master S1) with the row in it. The test of trust is that the no was written down and the offer was real.

Number to land: 3 orgs signed the row · 0 open ‘who owns this bucket’ (both user anchor)  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF I would open every no with the need I can say yes to and the number that makes the no checkable.  ·  Do NOT say: that Merchandising agreed happily; I do not know their private view.
Success and Scale Bring Broad Responsibility · AIQB Q5Q4. “Tell me about a time when you considered everyone who was affected by your work, not just your direct customers or people within your organization.”

An oversell looks like a ledger error to a planner and a broken promise to a customer. At the scale of this DC — an illustrative thirty million orders a year at a benchmark 0.10% oversell rate — that is thirty thousand customers a year who paid for something we did not have. I did not let the dollar comparison be the whole argument for the no on promisable reserve. The dollar figure — fifty to two hundred thousand per ten thousand reserved units — is the floor of the case; the rest is what it does to a customer’s trust in a promise. So the design treats a broken promise as a defect with an owner and a screen, not a variance to be tolerated. The record: customer-facing oversell incidents zero through two go-lives. At Amazon scale that is the difference between a rare event and a daily one.

Number to land: 30,000 incidents/yr (illustrative) → 0; zero through two go-lives  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF a promise date on a Prime order is the same contract; I would run the same defect-not-variance logic on the promise seam.  ·  Do NOT say: a real customer count; that 30,000 is measured — it is S1’s illustrative orders × benchmark rate.
Same story, three angles — data · conflict/influence/backbone · failure-learningNEW

Headline metric: visibility yes · promisability no · 3 orgs signed (user anchor)
Priced no; visibility offered; the no written as an FSD row; the comingle-reserve improvements held out and priced as a later enhancement.		Failure / learning angle · Earn Trust“I priced the no with a release rate I never measured.”
Headline metric: release rate 20% illustrative vs p* = 36.7%
The row in the FSD carried the decision, but not the measurement that would flip it: the historical release rate by SKU class. Do differently: put the ask for that measurement in the same row.
Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups, with numeric answersNEW
How do you know it was the right call and not just caution?

It is right as long as the release rate is below 36.7% (61.2% all-in). The record is zero oversell incidents; I cannot prove the counterfactual. The number that would change my mind is a measured release rate above the break-even with a hold-until date.

Say: “The decision is right below a 37% release rate; I did not measure it, so the row I would add is that measurement — if it came back above 37%, I would reopen it.”

Isn’t splitting the cost into $11 and $30 a way to get the answer you want?

No — the decision holds on both: −$50K and −$202K per 10,000 units at 20%. It flips only above p* in either view, and the two views bracket it between 36.7% and 61.2%.

Say: “I show both cost views because the answer does not depend on which you believe — it depends on whether the release rate is above 37% or 61%.”

Who bore the cost of your no?

Merchandising: for a period the reserve was visible but not sellable in the channel until released; the comingle-reserve improvements were deferred and priced at steering.

Say: “Merchandising bore it — visible but not sellable until release; the comingle-reserve improvements were held out of the release to protect zero incidents and priced, not promised.”

20-second version + NACF bridgeNEW

20-second version. At the Utah DC I said no to making merch reserve promisable — stock in the building but not for sale — by pricing the no: it pays only above about 37% release, which on a 20% illustrative rate is −$50K per 10,000 units. I offered visibility instead, wrote the no into the FSD, and customer-facing oversell went to zero through both go-lives.

NACF bridge. NACF has the same seam on the dock: units received but not yet stowed, quarantined or in transit are in the building and not pickable. The promise should read pickable; p* = cost ÷ (gain + cost) — INV-D’s 3 ÷ 12 = 25% shape — says when promising ahead of the stow pays. 10,000 reserved units at 0.25 ft³ = 2,500 ft³: CAP-C’s cube in miniature.

U4 — Where the node’s constraint actually bound — labor hours, then pick accuracy, then cube: one capacity envelope that priced the robotics throttle and the pooled spaceNEW

LPs this story serves here: Bias for Action (primary — the throttle decision the week the gate failed) · Frugality (price both sides; pay for the cheaper one) · Dive Deep (the constraint was accuracy, not robot speed) · Strive to be Earth’s Best Employer (the floor protected during the ramp)
Process family: Make / Deliver — DC capacity and throughput analysis: labor-hour envelope, automation ramp, constraint identification, cube
Honesty label. Record: robotics gate 98% published before the ramp; week two at 94%; bots throttled to ~30% of volume for a week; zone/slotting logic root-caused; full volume week three; +15% processing efficiency (Sephora-signed, measured by DC Ops); DC Ops owned the gate and the dial; peak (Oct–Dec) untouched. Modeled / illustrative (S2’s DC): 100,000 units/week, 120 FTE × 2,000 h, $28/h. Mine to label: $12 per mis-pick, linear hours-versus-bot-share, 10,000 attempted picks, $25 per unit and 0.25 ft³ per unit — all illustrative. I priced the trade; DC Ops ran the floor; I do not claim whose budget carried the hours.
Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each
Bias for Action. The week-2 read was 94 against a published 98; the dial, built in advance and owned by DC Ops, went to ~30% the same week — a reversible decision with a number.
Frugality. I priced both sides: ≈$11.8K of extra labor hours against ≈$50.4K of mis-pick exposure, and paid the cheaper one with existing labor — no new tooling.
Dive Deep. The robots could pick; they could not pick right — the binding constraint at week 2 was accuracy, root-caused to zone and slotting logic, not robot speed.
Strive to be Earth’s Best Employer. The throttle kept the floor from absorbing the mis-picks and the rework; the interim reporting burden was named as the price. No headcount or attrition figure is claimed.
60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Sephora’s Utah DC was ramping Locus robotics behind a published 98% pick-accuracy gate, on an illustrative 100,000-units-a-week floor running 4,615 labor hours a week. Week two read 94%. Task. Decide, with DC Ops, how much robot volume the floor could carry — and where the node’s constraint actually sat. Action. Analysis first: before the ramp the binding constraint was labor hours; in week two it had moved to accuracy, not robot speed. Four points of accuracy at full bot share is 6,000 mis-picks a week against a 2,000 budget. I priced the dial: a throttle to 30% keeps bot-side errors at 1,800, inside the full-volume budget — the maximum safe share is 33.3% — at a cost of about 421 extra labor hours, roughly $11,800, against about 4,200 mis-picks avoided, roughly $50,400 on an illustrative $12 each. DC Ops owned the dial before the ramp, so the rule fired and I did not improvise. Zone and slotting logic was root-caused and fixed; full volume in week three. Pooling had freed the safety stock the node did not need to hold — about $2.2M modeled, roughly 22,000 cubic feet on illustrative unit cube. Result. Primary metric: the gate held — 94 against 98 became full volume in week three, with +15% processing efficiency Sephora-signed and zero customer-facing incidents. The dollars are a model; the gate, the throttle and the +15% are the record.

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — One physical node, one robotics ramp, one published gate. The floor was running at baseline productivity (index 100); the plan was +15% at stabilization; the robots were the lever. The week-2 gate read 94 against 98 with the Oct–Dec peak freeze ahead. Everyone could see the gap; nobody had a number for what each response would cost.

T — Say where the constraint bound and what each dial setting cost, on one page, in the week it mattered — and make sure the rule that fired was the floor owner’s, not mine.

Baselines on the table: gate 98% (record) · week 2 = 94% (record) · 240,000 h/yr = 4,615 h/wk on an illustrative 100K-units/week node · target index 115 → 4,013 h/wk · full-share exposure 6,000 mis-picks vs a 2,000 budget · peak freeze Oct–Dec.

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery (3/29 → 4/30) — profiled 801/901; read the node’s labor and throughput as an envelope — hours per week, units per hour, what the floor could ship; success criteria included peak untouched. Exit bar: criteria signed by steering; every impacted system owned.
  • Plan & Design (5/15 → 8/14) — wrote the gate (98% pick-accurate completed ÷ attempted), the throttle dial and the weekly read in the council pack before the robots arrived; DC Ops took ownership of the gate and the dial. Exit bar: council 7/31; FSD 8/14; the dial owned before the ramp.
  • Build & Test (Aug → Jan/Apr) — the Locus gate definition and throttle spec in the build; the 3-day Locus workshop; RTL slipped three weeks, escalated the week it surfaced, re-slotted openly, UAT not compressed. Exit bar: Locus RTL retested after the slip; E2E days dated.
  • Cutover & Go-Live (January; May 1 → Jul 1) — ramp read weekly; week 2 = 94 against 98 → throttle to ~30% for a week → zone/slotting RCA → week-3 re-read at the same gate. Exit bar: rule fired; full volume week 3.
  • Hypercare / Close (Jun → Jul) — +15% measured post-stabilization by DC Ops; hypercare exit week 3 of 4; the dial and the gate handed to the next DC as a template. Exit bar: exit at < 2 blockers/day sustained.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the capacity envelope, the dial arithmetic and the go/no-go on the ramp read. DC Ops: the gate, the dial, the floor and the +15% measurement. Locus and platform teams: the build and the slotting fix.

Designed the process; others own it now. DC Ops owns the dial and the weekly read; the template went to the next DC. Trade-off put to leadership: a week of robot volume for a root-caused ramp — recommended with the arithmetic on both sides.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality
  • $ — modeled on S2’s illustrative DC: throttle premium ≈421 h × $28 = $11,799 against ≈4,200 mis-picks × $12 = $50,400 avoided; break-even mis-pick cost $2.81. Pooled safety stock released ≈$2.2M ≈ 22,000 ft³ (modeled). Not claimed as savings.
  • # — gate 98%, week 2 at 94%, throttle ~30% for one week, full volume week 3 (record); 4,615 → 4,013 labor hours/wk at +15% (modeled on the record index).
  • % — +15% processing efficiency (record, Sephora-signed); max safe bot share 33.3% against the ~30% dial (arithmetic).
  • Time — one week at ~30%; week-3 re-read at the same gate; hypercare exit week 3 of 4; ~92 days of float from Jul 1 to the Oct freeze (lower bound).
  • Customer — zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives; peak untouched (record).
  • Quality — the gate stayed a gate — it fired at 94 and was re-read at the same number; the cause (zone/slotting logic) was removed, not routed around.

Probing — five facets, then the hardest follow-upsNEW
The five facets — where each one lives in this story
1 · Data analysis. Grain: node × week × bot share. Data: labor hours, units, attempted and accurate picks, mis-pick cost. Cuts: accuracy by week; hours by share; exposure by share. Revealed: the constraint had moved from labor hours (pre-ramp) to accuracy (ramp) — robot speed was never the binding limit.
2 · Solution design. Options: (A) hold full volume and fix live; (B) throttle to 0 and re-test later; (C) throttle to ~30% and keep re-reading. Chose C — A’s exposure is 4,000 mis-picks over budget; B costs ≈$5.1K more and loses the live volume the gate reads.
3 · Math & data logic. hours(s) = H₀ − s(H₀ − H₁); premium = (1 − s)(H₀ − H₁); errors = 6% × s × volume; s_max = (1 − 0.98)/(1 − 0.94) = 33.3%; z = 0.04/√(0.98 × 0.02/n).
4 · Implementation. Gate and dial published and owned by DC Ops before the ramp; weekly read; RCA; re-read; hypercare exit. Risks: ramp with no stop condition, two live truths, peak collision.
5 · Probe questions. Below: why 30, why not 0, is 94 noise, where was the constraint, and the three trap drills.
Facet 5 — probe questions (challenge Q&A tuned to this interviewer)

Infra — why a 30% throttle, not 0% or 50%?

At 30% the bot-side errors are 1,800, inside the 2,000-per-week budget the gate allows at full volume; the maximum safe share is 33.3%. At 50% errors would be 3,000 — over. At 0% the premium rises to ≈$16.9K and the gate has no live bot volume to read.

Infra — is 94 versus 98 noise?

No. On an illustrative 10,000 attempted picks the standard error is 0.14 points and the gap is 28.6σ; even on 500 picks it is 6.4σ. The week-2 read was a real miss, not sampling.

Infra — where did the node’s constraint actually bind?

Pre-ramp: labor hours — 4,615 h a week for 100K units. In the ramp: accuracy, not robot speed — the zone/slotting logic. After: labor again, at +15% — 4,013 h. I did not claim space bound; I do not know the Utah cube utilization VERIFY-WITH-RAJESH.

Infra — what did the throttle cost, and who paid?

About 421 extra labor hours ≈ $11,799 on the illustrative $28 — about 10.5 FTE-weeks at 40 hours. I do not claim whose budget carried them; DC Ops ran the floor.

Infra — did the throttle create a backlog?

Not on this arithmetic: at fixed baseline hours the node’s capacity under a 30% share is ≈104,072 units a week against 100,000 — 4.1% headroom. It is a linear model; the real node’s hours were not fixed.

Spoken statement

“Before the robots arrived the node’s binding constraint was labor hours; in week two it was accuracy — 94 against a published 98 — not robot speed. On an illustrative hundred-thousand-unit week that is about 4,000 mis-picks over the gate’s budget at full share. A throttle to 30% kept the errors inside the budget, because the maximum safe share is 33%, at a cost of about 420 extra hours, roughly twelve thousand dollars, against about fifty thousand dollars of mis-picks avoided — modeled, on a twelve-dollar mis-pick I label illustrative. DC Ops owned the dial, so the rule fired; the zone and slotting logic was fixed and week three ran at full volume. The +15% and the zero incidents are the record.”

If challenged

“The $12 mis-pick cost is invented.”

Yes, illustrative — and the decision does not depend on it: the throttle pays above $2.81 per mis-pick, so the answer holds across any plausible cost. The record is the gate, the dial and the week-3 re-read.

“You only fixed it because the slotting logic was wrong — the throttle was irrelevant.”

Two levers: the throttle bounded exposure while the fix was made; the fix removed the cause. I cannot separate their contributions in the record, and I would not claim to — but the dial let the re-test run on live volume.

Behavioral pack — four separate 90-second STAR answers, each from a different moment of the storyNEW
Bias for Action · AIQB Q1Q1. “Give me an example of a calculated risk that you have taken where speed was critical.”

Week two of the robotics ramp read 94 against a published 98. The gate was published before the robots arrived, and DC Ops owned the throttle dial, so I did not need a meeting to decide whether to act; I needed to decide how far to turn the dial. I priced it that week: at full bot share four points of accuracy is about 6,000 mis-picks a week against a 2,000 budget; a throttle to 30% keeps the bot-side errors at 1,800, inside the budget — the maximum safe share is 33.3% — and costs about 420 extra labor hours. The decision was reversible: re-read the same gate in a week. I dialed to roughly 30%, root-caused the zone and slotting logic, and ran full volume in week three. Speed came from the decision being built before the pressure arrived.

Number to land: 94 vs 98 → ~30% dial → full volume week 3  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF the same pattern — a published ceiling, a pre-built reversible response, a weekly re-read — applies to a new FC’s inbound ramp.  ·  Do NOT say: that the robots failed; that the dial was my invention — DC Ops owned it.
Frugality · AIQB Q4Q2. “Tell me about a time when you had to make tradeoffs between quality and cost.”

I did not buy a fix; I priced a setting on a dial that already existed. The choice in week two was between running at full volume and absorbing the mis-picks, or slowing the bots and covering the volume with the floor’s existing labor. I put both on one page: the throttle costs about 421 hours — roughly eleven thousand eight hundred dollars at an illustrative $28 — and avoids about 4,200 mis-picks, which is fifty thousand dollars at twelve dollars each, or any cost above about two dollars eighty. That is 4.3 to 1 on illustrative inputs. No new tooling, no new heads; the dial existed because DC Ops and I had built it before the ramp. And the pooled safety stock freed about 22,000 cubic feet of space the node no longer had to hold. The record is the gate holding and the +15%; the dollars are my model and I say so.

Number to land: ≈$11.8K vs ≈$50.4K (4.3×); break-even $2.81  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF a node’s constraint is priced the same way — flex labor against dwell, CAP-B and CAP-E — before any new capacity is bought.  ·  Do NOT say: that the $12 or the $28 is measured; that this was a saving.
Dive Deep · AIQB Q3Q3. “Tell me about a problem you had to solve that required in-depth thought and analysis.”

Everyone assumed the question was how fast the robots could go. The data said the constraint had moved. Before the ramp the node was bound by labor hours — 4,615 a week for 100,000 units. In week two the robots were picking; they were not picking accurately: 94 against 98. At a full bot share that is thousands of mis-picks, and more robot speed would have made it worse. I asked what was different about the misses, and the answer was the zone and slotting logic — the cause was in how work was assigned, not in the robots. So the fix went to the logic, and the throttle bought the time. After the fix and the week-three re-read at the same gate, the constraint went back to labor hours — now at +15%, about 4,013 a week. The mistake I avoided was optimizing the non-binding constraint.

Number to land: constraint: labor → accuracy → labor; 94 vs 98; +15%  ·  Pivot to NACF: NACF’s capacity chain is the same — doors, UPH, cube, dwell — and the first job is to find which one binds this week.  ·  Do NOT say: that I fixed the slotting logic myself; the platform team did.
Strive to be Earth's Best Employer · AIQB Q3Q4. “Describe a time when you implemented a change at work aimed at improving the work experience of yourself or coworkers.”

The floor was the one place the cost of a bad ramp would land. If the bots had stayed at full volume at 94% accuracy, every mis-pick becomes rework for someone on the floor, during a go-live. I wrote the gate and the dial before the ramp, and DC Ops owned them — so the people who knew the floor held the stop condition, not a program manager on a schedule. When the gate fired, the throttle kept the mis-picks and the rework off the floor for a week while the logic was fixed. I also named the price of the interim to leadership: for four months every report had to declare which system of record it read. I will not quote a headcount or an attrition number — there is none in the record — but the design principle is the stop condition belongs to the people who carry the consequence.

Number to land: the dial owned by DC Ops before the ramp  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF the same principle applies to a peak ramp — the dock and the floor own the stop condition for their own safety and quality.  ·  Do NOT say: a headcount, an attrition or a safety figure — none exists in the record.
Same story, three angles — data · conflict/influence/backbone · failure-learningNEW
Data angle · Dive Deep“Four points of accuracy is 6,000 mis-picks a week at full share against a 2,000 budget.”
Headline metric: s_max = 33.3% vs ~30% dial; 28.6σ
The throttle share has a maximum that falls out of the gate (0.02/0.06). The record’s ~30% sits just inside it. The misses were not noise (28.6σ on an illustrative 10,000 attempts). Honest limit: the volume and mis-pick cost are illustrative.		Conflict / influence angle · Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit“Letting a 94% system carry full volume was the path of least resistance; the gate was published before that temptation existed.”
Headline metric: 94 vs 98; dial ~30%
Holding the gate was enforcement, not escalation; the trade was visible in dollars on both sides. Record: the alternative — a 94% system carrying full volume into the combined go-live — is how an incident with a robot in the photo happens.		Failure / learning angle · Ownership“I had the Locus RTL on the plan as a task, not as a risk.”
Headline metric: 3-week RTL slip; 94 vs 98
The slip was escalated the week it surfaced and re-slotted openly without compressing UAT, but it had no trigger or owner on the register at the start. Do differently: put vendor RTL on the register as a risk from day one, and publish the zone/slotting test as a gate prerequisite.
Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups, with numeric answersNEW
How do you know the throttle — and not the slotting fix — got you to 98?

I don’t, and I would not claim to. The throttle bounded exposure at 1,800 errors against a 2,000 budget while the fix was made; the fix removed the cause; the week-3 re-read at the same gate is the evidence of the combination.

Say: “I can’t separate them; the throttle bought exposure control and the time to test the fix on live volume, and the week-3 re-read is the evidence of both.”

Why not 0%? Why not 50%?

At 50% errors are 3,000 against 2,000 — over budget. At 0% the premium is ≈$16.9K instead of ≈$11.8K and the gate has no bot volume to read. 30% sits inside the 33.3% maximum.

Say: “Thirty is just inside the maximum safe share of thirty-three; fifty breaks the error budget and zero costs five thousand more with nothing to re-read.”

What is the number that would have changed your mind?

A mis-pick cost below $2.81: the throttle would not pay on the arithmetic. Or a re-read at week 3 still below 98: the dial stays, and the RCA reopens.

Say: “Below about three dollars a mis-pick the throttle doesn’t pay, and a week-three read under 98 would have kept the dial down.”

20-second version + NACF bridgeNEW

20-second version. In week two of Sephora’s robotics ramp the gate read 94 against a published 98; the constraint had moved from labor hours to pick accuracy, and a pre-built, DC-Ops-owned throttle to about 30% — inside the 33.3% maximum safe share — cost about 421 labor hours against roughly 4,200 mis-picks avoided, and full volume ran in week three.

NACF bridge. NACF’s inbound capacity chain has the same shape: CAP-A doors held at an 85% ceiling because above it dwell goes about 3×, CAP-B’s learning curve costing 13.6% more hours — the throttle week was +10.5% — and CAP-E’s ~14 flex heads. The throttle’s 421 hours is about ten and a half people for a week; the question for any new FC ramp is which of doors, UPH or cube binds this week, and who owns the dial.

U5 — The 343-task cutover runbook with one irreversible instant — priced rollback triggers, a three-SKU zero-then-publish trace, and what a week of slip is worthNEW

Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each
Ownership. The go/no-go recommendation, the runbook and the rollback triggers were mine; I wrote the recommendation off counted rows and I would have called no-go on one open Blocker.
Deliver Results. A 343-task, ~815-hour runbook ran inside a 61-day window with the peak freeze untouched: two go-lives, zero customer-facing incidents, hypercare exit week 3 of 4.

60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Sephora’s Utah DC cutover moved warehouse execution from two systemic warehouses to one comingle pool between May 1 and July 1, with zero customer-facing incidents allowed, an October–December peak freeze behind it, and about a dozen impacted systems in the path. Task. Own the runbook and the recommendation — what has to happen, in what order, who does it, and what makes us stop. Action. I built a 343-task, 48,885-minute — about 815-hour — runbook, each task with an owner, a duration and a predecessor; found the single irreversible instant, step 4, zero-then-publish, so no SKU was ever sellable on both selling locations at once; and wrote the rollback triggers as numbers per interface: sync below 99% for four consecutive hours, or order-flow p95 above twice the budget for an hour, consumers reverting to the prior publish. I wrote the go/no-go off counted rows — Blocker plus Critical at zero, mock three inside the 0.5% tolerance, runbook rehearsed — and I would have said no-go on one open Blocker. Hypercare was read as a decaying monthly arc of new issue rows, 271 → 65 → 21 → 14 → 4, and exited at under two blockers a day sustained. Result. Primary metric: zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives, rollback armed twice and never fired. The runbook cost about $90,000 of effort at an illustrative $110 an hour — less than three weeks of the modeled benefit it protected.

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — A multi-system cutover in a fixed window: BlueYonder upgrade, comingle reserve rules, robotics ramp and about a dozen impacted systems moving the same two months, with the peak freeze behind it and no room for a customer-facing incident. The interim ran two live truths.

T — Write the runbook that makes the cutover a sequence of owned tasks, identify the instant that cannot be undone, and fix the stop conditions in numbers before anyone is tired.

Baselines on the table: 343 tasks · 48,885 task-minutes ≈ 815 h · 61 days · one irreversible instant · rollback triggers per interface · mock 3 ≤ 0.5% · Blocker + Critical open = 0 · hypercare exit at < 2 blockers/day.

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery (3/29 → 4/30) — systems-impacted table with owner and written LOE per row; the interim-state risks named — two live truths, a ramp with no stop condition, a peak collision. Exit bar: every impacted system named to an owner (LOE landed 7/2).
  • Plan & Design (5/15 → 8/14) — staged two-go-live timeline; peak-freeze boundary decided; the six invariants and six-step choreography drafted; rollback concept — consumers revert to the prior publish. Exit bar: council 7/31; FSD 8/14; freeze boundary signed.
  • Build & Test (Aug → Jan/Apr) — three mock cutovers against the 0.5% tolerance; runbook tasks authored with owner, duration, predecessor; E2E days; the shadow monitor for the interim copy built and tested. Exit bar: mock 3 inside tolerance; E2E green three dates; B + C open = 0.
  • Cutover & Go-Live (January; May 1 → Jul 1) — executed the runbook: clear negatives, transfer AFS to Uncommitted, scan-and-reserve, zero-then-publish; inbound re-pointed W801 → W901 per shipment in Proship/TMS; triggers armed per interface. Exit bar: six invariants held; rollback never fired.
  • Hypercare / Close (Jun → Jul) — daily issue read; weekly Locus gate read; control-owner handoff; hypercare exit memo; template handed to the next DC. Exit bar: < 2 blockers/day sustained → exit week 3 of 4.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the runbook, the invariants, the choreography, the trigger numbers and the go/no-go recommendation. Executed by: platform, BlueYonder, Locus and DC Ops teams, each owning their tasks. QA owned the gate; steering signed the criteria.

Designed the process; others own it now. The runbook format (owner · duration · predecessor), the invariants and the numeric hypercare exit are the template handed to the next DC. Trade-off put to leadership: the date versus the bar — a counted go/no-go rather than a feeling, and a second cutover rather than two defect classes in one week.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality
  • $ — modeled: runbook effort 814.75 h × $110 = $89,622; one week of the modeled ≈$1.65M/yr benefit ≈ $31,731/wk → the runbook costs ≈2.8 weeks of what it protected. Not claimed as a saving.
  • # — 343 tasks / 48,885 task-minutes ≈ 815 h; 61-day window; 836-row issues log, 85.4% closed; monthly new-row arc 271 → 65 → 21 → 14 → 4 (record, from the log).
  • % — new-row arc −98.5% from June to October; sync trigger < 99% for 4 h; p95 trigger > 2× for 1 h (record).
  • Time — May 1 → Jul 1; hypercare exit week 3 of 4; peak freeze Oct–Dec untouched (record).
  • Customer — zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives; rollback armed twice, never fired (record).
  • Quality — six invariants held; one irreversible instant identified; Blocker + Critical open = 0 at go/no-go.

Probing — five facets, then the hardest follow-upsNEW
The five facets — where each one lives in this story
1 · Data analysis. Grain: task × owner × duration × predecessor; SKU × selling location at cutover; issue × severity × day. Cuts: task-hours by day; blockers by day; negatives by SKU. Revealed: the cutover had many tasks and one irreversible instant.
2 · Solution design. Options: (A) one big-bang go-live; (B) two go-lives each carrying one defect class (chosen); (C) a parallel run. A mixes defect classes; C leaves two truths live. B costs a second cutover and buys single-cause diagnosis.
3 · Math & data logic. Σ AFS_800 + Σ AFS_900 conserved; min(AFS_800, AFS_900) = 0 at every instant; triggers: sync < 99% × 4 h → ≈24 events ≈ $714 exposure; p95 > 2× × 1 h; persistence: q⁴ vs q.
4 · Implementation. Runbook of 343 tasks; six-step choreography; per-shipment inbound re-pointing; rollback per interface; daily issue read; control-owner handoff. Day-one pace planned in units — 280,000 units, 40 stores, 7 inbound appointments (user anchor, loop packet only) — and a discovery curve of 68 → 160 → 34 issues a week (record, computed from the log).
5 · Probe questions. Below: what made you say go, what did the triggers cost, what’s irreversible, why two go-lives, and the three trap drills.
Facet 5 — probe questions (challenge Q&A tuned to this interviewer)

Infra — what made you say go?

Counted rows: Blocker + Critical at zero, mock 3 inside 0.5%, runbook rehearsed, rollback armed per interface. I would have said no-go on one open Blocker; I never had to.

Infra — what is irreversible and how did you handle it?

Step 4, zero-then-publish: AFS_800 set to zero, then the same AFS published at 900. The reversible steps came first (clear negatives, transfer to Uncommitted, scan-and-reserve) so by step 4 the only open question was the publish.

Infra — what do the triggers cost if they are wrong?

On illustrative volume ≈595 units/h, 1% unsynced for 4 h is ≈23.8 events ≈ $714 of oversell exposure; a p95 at 120 s instead of 60 s for an hour adds a 60 s staleness window ≈ 9.9 units ≈ $298. Small against the cost of a false rollback, which is why the persistence requirement matters.

Infra — why two go-lives?

So we never hunted two defect classes in the same week. January was the planning go-live — purchasing and inbound, reserve rules live against demand, the floor untouched; May left warehouse execution as the only class.

Infra — what did the interim cost?

For four months every report had to declare which system of record it read — the price of two live truths. I would staff the interim reporting workstream on day one, not month three.

Spoken statement

“The cutover was 343 tasks and one irreversible instant. I put every control at that instant — clear the negatives, transfer to uncommitted, scan-and-reserve, then zero the old selling location and publish the new one — so no SKU was ever sellable in two places and the worst case was a sixty-second false zero, which costs about a sixth of a double-sell on S1’s benchmarks. The triggers were numbers per interface, and the persistence requirement is why they would not false-fire. I wrote the go/no-go off counted rows; the record is zero customer-facing incidents and a rollback that was armed twice and never needed. The runbook cost about $90,000 of effort at an illustrative rate — under three weeks of the benefit it protected.”

If challenged

“The rollback never fired — how do you know it works?”

I don’t know it end to end under load, and I will say so. What the record has is that the triggers were armed per interface, the failback concept was consumers revert to the prior publish, and the three mocks ran against the tolerance. The right do-differently is a timed rollback drill in mock three.

“Why four hours?”

Persistence. At an illustrative 2% healthy per-hour breach, four consecutive hours is 1.6 × 10⁻⁷ per window; one hour would false-fire about 29 times over the cutover. The assumption is independence — correlated outages break it — so the p95 trigger is the fast path.
