Behavioral pack — four separate 90-second STAR answers, each from a different moment of the storyNEW
Ownership · AIQB Q4Q1. “Tell me about a time when you made a hard decision to sacrifice short term gain for something that would create long term value for the business.”

I recommended giving up speed for certainty — two go-lives and a four-month bake — and wrote the go/no-go off counted rows rather than confidence. By the time of the May cutover Blocker plus Critical open was zero, mock three was inside the 0.5% tolerance, the 343-task runbook had been rehearsed, and rollback was armed per interface. I would have called no-go on one open Blocker, and I meant it. The runbook had one irreversible instant, step four, zero-then-publish, and I put every control at that instant: negatives cleared first, AFS transferred to uncommitted, scan-and-reserve, then zero the old selling location and publish the new. When something needed to be decided, it was decided against a number I had written before anyone was tired. The result was zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives and rollback armed twice, never fired. The interim reporting burden — four months of every report declaring its system of record — was a cost I should have staffed earlier, and I say that too.

Number to land: 343 tasks · 1 irreversible instant · 0 incidents · rollback armed twice, never fired  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF the owner of a new-FC launch writes the go/no-go off counted rows — doors tested, appointments scheduled, UPH validated — and the stop conditions before the first live trailer.  ·  Do NOT say: that I executed the tasks; that the rollback was proven under load.
Deliver Results · AIQB Q1Q2. “Give me an example of a time when you were able to deliver an important project under a tight deadline.”

The window was 61 days, May 1 to July 1, with the October to December peak freeze behind it — so slipping was not free. I wrote the runbook as 343 owned tasks, about 815 hours, each with a predecessor so the critical path was visible. A week of slip was worth about $31,700 of deferred benefit on S2’s modeled $1.65 million a year; the whole runbook cost about $90,000 of effort, under three weeks of that. So when the Locus RTL slipped three weeks I escalated the week it surfaced and re-slotted rather than letting it sit. Two go-lives landed on their dates, the monthly count of new issue rows fell 271 to 65 to 21 to 14 to 4, and hypercare exited in week three of four at under two blockers a day. The +15% processing efficiency was measured post-stabilization by DC Ops. The date held because every task had an owner and a predecessor.

Number to land: 61 days · 815 h · new rows 271 → 4 a month · exit week 3 of 4  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF a new-FC date lives or dies on the same arithmetic — doors, hours, appointments — written as owned tasks.  ·  Do NOT say: that the $31.7K is banked; that the $90K runbook cost is a record.
Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit · AIQB Q5Q3. “Tell me about a time when you pushed back against a decision that negatively impacted your team.”

The Locus RTL slipped three weeks, and the easy way to absorb it was to compress UAT. I refused. I escalated the slip the week it surfaced — not when it hurt — re-slotted the plan openly so everyone saw the new dates, and held UAT at its full length; the robotics were retested after the slip. My argument was the bar itself: Blocker plus Critical open had to be zero at go/no-go, and compressing UAT would have moved findings from the test room to the floor. One open Blocker is a no-go; I never had to call it, because the bar was written before the pressure arrived.

Number to land: RTL slipped 3 weeks · UAT not compressed · B + C open = 0  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF the same line applies to a launch date — what is the bar, in numbers, that a slipped vendor does not get to lower.  ·  Do NOT say: that I overruled steering; the dates were re-slotted openly, not forced.
Dive Deep · AIQB Q6Q4. “Have you ever created a metric that helped identify a need for a change in your department?”

I did the arithmetic on the thresholds instead of accepting round numbers. The rollback trigger — sync below 99% for four consecutive hours — sounds arbitrary until you price it: on an illustrative 100,000-unit week, 1% unsynced for four hours is about 24 events, roughly $714 of oversell exposure at $30 each. The four-hour persistence is why it does not false-fire: at an illustrative 2% healthy breach, four consecutive hours is one in six million per window; a single hour would false-fire about 29 times across the cutover. And I traced three SKUs through the irreversible step to prove the invariant: the units across both selling locations sum to the same 680 before and after, and no SKU is sellable on both. The false-zero window costs about a sixth of a double-sell. Detail, not drama.

Number to land: ≈24 events ≈ $714; 0.02⁴ vs 0.02; 680 conserved  ·  Pivot to NACF: NACF dock and UPH plans run on the same detail — appointment slots and dwell — and I would price every threshold the same way.  ·  Do NOT say: that the exposure numbers are measured; they are illustrative on 100K units/week.

Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups, with numeric answersNEW
How do you know the cutover worked because of the runbook and not because the team was good?

I can’t separate them, and a good team is part of the design: each task had an owner, duration and predecessor. The measurable thing is the invariants held (Σ AFS conserved, no SKU sellable twice) and the monthly new-row arc 271 → 4.

Say: “The runbook’s job was to make good people’s work sequenceable; the evidence is that the invariants held and the monthly new-row arc went 271 to 4 — not that the team was lucky.”

Why not one go-live and save the second cutover?

The second cutover buys single-cause diagnosis: in a one-go-live world every issue triages against two hypotheses. The cost of the second go-live is real and in the record: a deliberate four-month bake running two systems of record, with daily reconciliation (Apple C1). The decision is qualitative — triage time, not fix time, is what a 160-issue week costs, and the issues log confirms that week: 68, then 160, then 34 identified in the weeks of June 9, 16 and 23 (computed from the file).

Say: “I paid for a second cutover to keep diagnosis univariate; that is a judgment call, and I put the price on the table at steering.”

What would have changed your mind on go?

One open Blocker; mock 3 outside 0.5%; a sync read below 99% in the rehearsal; or a rollback I couldn’t describe in a sentence per interface.

Say: “Any one of four numbers — one open Blocker, mock three over half a percent, sync under 99%, or a rollback I couldn’t state per interface.”

20-second version + NACF bridgeNEW

20-second version. I owned the Utah DC cutover runbook — 343 tasks, about 815 hours, a 61-day window — with one irreversible instant, zero-then-publish, rollback triggers written as numbers per interface (sync under 99% for four hours; p95 over twice budget for an hour), and a go/no-go off counted rows; zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives and rollback never needed.

NACF bridge. NACF’s new-FC launch has the same irreversible instant — the first live inbound appointment — and I would write it the same way: an owned-task runbook, a numeric go/no-go, and stop conditions before the first trailer. At Sephora the dock-side version was re-pointing each inbound shipment’s warehouse ID W801 → W901 in Proship/TMS, shipment by shipment; at NACF the CAP-A 85% door ceiling is the slack that absorbs a re-pointed or late trailer.

U6 — Negative AFS and the AFS Correction screen — detecting an over-committed SKU, root-causing it to one of three seams, and giving every AFS-changing act a screen, a role and an audit rowNEW

LPs this story serves here: Dive Deep (primary — decomposing a negative to its cause class) · Ownership (the control-owner map handed off) · Customer Obsession (a negative is a promise we cannot keep) · Insist on the Highest Standards (drift tolerance zero; negatives clean at T+1)
Process family: Deliver / Return — inventory-accuracy control: exception detection, root cause by cause class, control-owner governance
Honesty label. Record: AFS = Basis − PickRes − EcomPick − EcomPack − SNB; negatives cleared first at cutover; Negative AFS clean at T+1 was a go-live exit bar; daily ISM-vs-MB52 drift with tolerance 0 — a non-zero drift is a Sev, not a planning variance; control-owner map: Negative AFS → Inventory Control, Internal Audit and Finance; AFS Correction → ISM app team; the daily drift report → Inventory Control; every AFS-changing act had a screen, a role and an audit row; the register’s top three risks — vocabulary drift, feed latency from WMS/OMS/POS, override abuse on AFS-changing screens — are my three cause classes. Illustrative: the −10 SKU, its 5/3/2 split, $30 per incident, the σ = 10 sanity check. Record-from-docs (issues-log file, Comments column): row ID 716 and row ID 818 — the only two of 836 rows that name AFS. I defined the screens, owners and cause classes; ISM app and platform teams built them; the control owners run them.
Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each
Dive Deep. A negative AFS is a symptom; I decomposed it into three cause classes — event cardinality, feed latency, override — each with a different fix and a different owner.
Ownership. Every AFS-changing act had a screen, a role and an audit row before I stepped back; the control owners ran the Negative AFS report without me.
Customer Obsession. A negative AFS is units already promised that the floor cannot back; I treated it as a customer defect with a clock, not a variance.
Insist on the Highest Standards. Drift tolerance was zero — a non-zero drift is a Sev — and the exit bar was zero SKUs of drift on consecutive days, with negatives clean at T+1.
60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Once Sephora’s Utah DC became one comingled pool with one published AFS per node per channel, there was one new failure mode: a SKU whose committed buckets exceed what the floor can back — a negative AFS, units we have already promised and cannot ship. Task. Own how that is detected, root-caused and fixed, and who is allowed to change an AFS at all. Action. I defined the screens and the control-owner map before go-live: the Negative AFS report to Inventory Control, Internal Audit and Finance; the AFS Correction screen to the ISM app team; the daily drift report, ISM-vs-MB52, to Inventory Control, with tolerance zero — a non-zero drift is a Sev, not a planning variance. Every AFS-changing act had a screen, a role and an audit row. Root cause went by cause class — the register’s top three: event cardinality, feed latency, override — so a worked SKU at minus ten decomposes into a duplicate event, a lagging WMS feed and a manual override, each with its own owner. The asymmetry behind it: oversell costs about $30 and an undersold unit about $4.75, a 6.3-to-1 ratio, so the published number sits near the 14th percentile of true availability — and the lower-of-two basis gets within a unit of that. Result. Primary metric: customer-facing oversell incidents zero through both go-lives; negatives clean at T+1 at cutover; drift at zero SKUs on consecutive days at hypercare exit; the control owners running the report without me.

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — A comingled pool publishes one AFS per node per channel and the OMS reads it, never computes it. When committed buckets exceed Basis, AFS goes negative: units promised that the floor cannot back. Before ISM, two books were reconciled by hand — about 4 FTE — and a negative surfaced as an order that could not ship.

T — Design detection, cause-class root-causing and ownership for exceptions that change AFS — so that a negative is found, explained, fixed and audited by the people who own the consequence, not by me.

Baselines on the table: ≈4 FTE reconciling two books by hand · drift unmeasured → tolerance 0 · negatives clean at T+1 as a go-live bar · the register’s top-three risks (vocabulary drift, feed latency, override abuse) each with a trigger, a mitigation and an owner — mine was drift.

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery (3/29 → 4/30) — bucket inventory with one owner per bucket; where in the old world negatives appeared and who chased them; sessions closed 4/30. Exit bar: every bucket owned by one system; open questions dated.
  • Plan & Design (5/15 → 8/14) — wrote AFS = Basis − buckets; asymmetry (oversell customer defect / undersell planning variance); drift tolerance 0; screens and roles; the three cause classes from the risk register. Exit bar: FSD signed 8/14; every AFS-changing act has a screen and a role.
  • Build & Test (Aug → Jan/Apr) — matrix cells for negatives, replays and overrides (U1); AFS Correction audit-row assertions; SIT contract defects closed before UAT. Exit bar: Blocker + Critical open = 0; E2E days dated.
  • Cutover & Go-Live (January; May 1 → Jul 1) — step 1 of the May choreography: clear negatives first; Negative AFS clean at T+1; the screens live with control owners assigned. Exit bar: negatives clean at T+1; no SKU with two published AFS values.
  • Hypercare / Close (Jun → Jul) — daily drift report; Negative AFS / AFS Correction audit review; control-owner handoff; exit into the monthly release train. Exit bar: drift = 0 SKUs on consecutive days; owners running the report without me.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the cause classes, the screens’ definitions, the owner map, the tolerance. ISM app and platform teams built the screens; Inventory Control, Internal Audit and Finance run Negative AFS; the ISM app team runs AFS Correction.

Designed the process; others own it now. Control owners and the monthly release train own the screens now. Trade-off put to leadership: visible undersell and a zero-tolerance drift — more investigations — against oversell as a customer defect; I accepted the investigations.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality
  • $ — modeled from S1: reconciliation 4 → 1 FTE = 3 × $110K = $330K/yr; baseline oversell exposure 30,000/365 = 82.2 incidents/day × $30 = $2,466/day on illustrative orders. Not claimed as savings; the record movement is incidents → zero.
  • # — negatives clean at T+1 at cutover; drift = 0 SKUs on consecutive days; 3 cause classes; 3 control-owner orgs + the ISM app team (record).
  • % — asymmetry 1 : 6.3; critical ratio 13.7% (modeled on S1’s benchmark).
  • Time — T+1 at cutover; hypercare exit week 3 of 4; FSD signed 8/14 (record).
  • Customer — customer-facing oversell incidents zero through both go-lives (record).
  • Quality — every AFS-changing act has a screen, a role and an audit row; drift tolerance 0 (record design).

Probing — five facets, then the hardest follow-upsNEW
The five facets — where each one lives in this story
1 · Data analysis. Grain: SKU × node × channel × bucket × time. Data: ISM buckets, MB52 snapshot, the audit rows — and, for the legacy-side evidence, the 836-row WMS/Locus issues log: a keyword search of its five text columns finds only two rows that name AFS. Row ID 716 (Critical, IC): ‘Dept 96 has 1800 SKUs. 400 are − AFS in MCS and 0 in BY’ — 22.2% of that department’s SKUs negative in the legacy book and none in the WMS. Row ID 818 (Critical): inventory holds validated moving units ‘from AFS to Merch Res after about 10min’ — a latency datum inside the ≤15-minute feed budget. Cuts: negatives by cause class. Revealed: the WMS defect book is nearly silent on AFS, so exceptions to the published number needed their own controls in ISM/S/4.
2 · Solution design. Options: (A) reconcile by hand (4 FTE); (B) a daily report with no owners; (C) a screen + role + audit row per AFS-changing act with a cause class (chosen). A and B leave exceptions unowned.
3 · Math & data logic. AFS = 25 − (12 + 10 + 8 + 5) = −10; critical ratio = $4.75 ÷ ($4.75 + $30) = 13.7%; z = -1.10; buffer = 1.10 × σ(10) = 11.0; lower-of-two sits 10 below the mean of the books (990 → 980); drift = 957 − 957 = 0.
4 · Implementation. Screens and owners at design; clear negatives first at cutover; T+1; daily drift at hypercare; handoff and exit memo. Risks: vocabulary drift, feed latency, override abuse.
5 · Probe questions. Below: how do you root-cause a negative, why tolerance zero, what the screen cannot see, who owns what, and the three trap drills.
Facet 5 — probe questions (challenge Q&A tuned to this interviewer)

Infra — a SKU shows −10. What do you do?

Decompose by cause class. Cardinality: an event in two buckets (a duplicate SNB) — fix the event, replay is a no-op, add a matrix cell. Latency: a WMS adjustment still inside the ≤15-minute feed plus under-5-minute consumer lag — wait and re-read. Override: a manual AFS change — check role and audit row. On the illustrative split 5/3/2 the first fix takes the −10 to −5.

Infra — why drift tolerance of zero?

Because the books should agree and a difference means one of them is wrong; a non-zero drift is a Sev, not a planning variance. It also made the hypercare exit crisp: zero SKUs of drift on consecutive days. If I had to set a non-zero tolerance I would price it: each unit is up to $30.

Infra — what can the Negative AFS screen not see?

Undersell. It is a one-sided detector: a SKU showing less than the floor could sell never goes negative. The asymmetry makes that deliberate, but it means a wrong rule that starves a channel (my first in-transit rule, master S4) is invisible to it.

Infra — who owns what?

Negative AFS: Inventory Control, Internal Audit and Finance. AFS Correction: the ISM app team. The daily drift report: Inventory Control. Every AFS-changing act has a screen, a role and an audit row; override abuse was the register’s third risk.

Infra — why is the lower-of-two the right buffer?

Sanity check, not a derivation: a critical ratio of 13.7% puts the published number about 1.10σ under the mean; if the two books’ half-gap (10) is one σ, that is ≈11.0 units — and min(1,000, 980) sits 10 under the books’ mean of 990.

Spoken statement

“A negative AFS is units we have already promised and the floor cannot back. I did not treat it as one thing: it decomposes into three seams — an event that landed in two buckets, a feed that has not caught up, and a manual override — and each has a different fix and a different owner. Inventory Control, Internal Audit and Finance run the Negative AFS report; the ISM app team runs the AFS Correction screen; every AFS-changing act has a screen, a role and an audit row. The drift tolerance is zero because a difference means one book is wrong. The record is negatives clean at T+1, drift at zero on consecutive days at hypercare exit, and zero customer-facing oversell incidents through both go-lives; the dollar view is an asymmetry — about six to one — on benchmark inputs.”

If challenged

“The 5/3/2 split is made up.”

It is illustrative, tagged, and I would not quote it. What is record is the three cause classes — they are the register’s top three risks — and the owner for each. The real split is a pull from the audit rows.

“How do you know the controls — not the matrix — kept incidents at zero?”

I can’t isolate them. The controls and the matrix are two halves: the matrix proves the events are mapped; the screens catch what the mapping did not. The record is the combination: zero incidents, drift at zero, negatives clean.

Behavioral pack — four separate 90-second STAR answers, each from a different moment of the storyNEW
Dive Deep · AIQB Q2Q1. “Tell me about a situation that required you to dig deep to get to the root cause.”

The recurring problem was a negative available-to-sell — units promised that the floor could not back — and the first instinct is to fix the SKU. I looked at what a negative actually is: committed buckets exceeding the supply basis. Take a worked SKU with a basis of 25 and 35 units committed: minus ten. I decomposed it into three cause classes — the register’s top three: an event that landed in two buckets, a feed that had not caught up, and a manual override. A duplicate event in the shipped-not-billed bucket takes the minus ten to minus five; a warehouse adjustment still inside the fifteen-minute feed takes it to minus two; an unauthorized override, removed through the AFS Correction screen with a role and an audit row, takes it to zero. Each cause class has a different fix and a different owner, so the fix is at the seam, not the SKU. That is why the matrix has a cell for each, and why the control owners had a cause class to write on every audit row.

Number to land: −10 → −5 → −2 → 0 (illustrative); 3 cause classes  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF, a negative-available ASIN decomposes the same way — a double receive scan, dock-to-stock lag, a manual adjustment — and I would ask which seam first.  ·  Do NOT say: that the 5/3/2 split is measured; that I built the screens.
Ownership · AIQB Q5Q2. “Describe a time when you had to transition a project you owned to a new owner.”

I did not finish the program until the exceptions had owners who did not need me. Before go-live I mapped every AFS-changing act to a screen, a role and an audit row, and every exception report to an owner: the Negative AFS report to Inventory Control, Internal Audit and Finance; the AFS Correction screen to the ISM app team; the daily ISM-versus-MB52 drift report to Inventory Control. In hypercare the test was whether the control owners ran the report without me — part of the exit bar, along with drift at zero SKUs on consecutive days and blockers under two a day. Then the design entered the monthly release train, so a new node or channel is a matrix row and an owner row, not a project. I measure ownership by whether the process survived my leaving.

Number to land: 3 owner orgs + ISM app team; drift 0 on consecutive days  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF I would hand a launch’s exception reports to named owners with a screen, a role and an audit row before the first live trailer.  ·  Do NOT say: that I run the screens now; the control owners do.
Customer Obsession · AIQB Q8Q3. “Tell me about a time when you had to balance the needs of the customer with the needs of the business.”

The convenient metric was a clean-looking available number; the customer’s interest was a promise we could keep. A negative AFS is a customer we have already told yes. So the policy is an asymmetry: an oversell is a customer defect, about thirty dollars on benchmark inputs; an undersell is a planning variance, about five. That is roughly six to one, and the asymmetry decides the design — the supply basis is the lower of the two books, so the published number sits conservatively under the truth. The price is visible undersell: some sellable stock shows as unavailable, and Inventory Control chases it. I accepted that on purpose. A sanity check supports it: a six-to-one asymmetry puts the right published number about one standard deviation below the mean, and the lower-of-two sits within about a unit of that on the worked SKU. The record is customer-facing oversell incidents going to zero through both go-lives.

Number to land: 1 : 6.3 asymmetry; critical ratio 13.7%; incidents → zero  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF the promise date on an order is the same commitment — I would hold a conservative buffer against a missed date on the same arithmetic.  ·  Do NOT say: that undersell is free; that 6.3 is measured — it rests on S1’s benchmark and a 25% assumption.
Insist on the Highest Standards · AIQB Q1Q4. “Describe a time when you refused to compromise your standards around quality/customer service, etc.”

I set the drift tolerance at zero, and people reasonably asked whether that was realistic. The daily check is ISM’s copy of S/4 available against the MB52 snapshot, and a non-zero drift is a Sev, not a planning variance, because two books that should agree and don’t mean one of them is wrong — and you do not know which one a customer will meet. The cost is more investigations — the trade I accepted. I made the standard checkable: negatives clean at T+1 at cutover, drift zero SKUs on consecutive days as the hypercare exit, and blockers under two a day. It held because the exit was a number nobody could negotiate. The thing I would say honestly is that zero tolerance is a posture, not a guarantee — it is only as good as the cause class written on each audit row.

Number to land: drift tolerance 0; exit: 0 SKUs on consecutive days  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF I would set the same kind of zero-tolerance reconciliation on dock-received versus system-available counts, with a number for the exit.  ·  Do NOT say: that zero tolerance is easy or free; that it guarantees correctness.

Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups, with numeric answersNEW
How do you know the Negative AFS screen is why incidents went to zero?

I can’t isolate it. The matrix proves the events are mapped; the screens catch what the mapping missed; drift at zero is the check that books agree. The record is the combination.

Say: “I can’t separate the screens from the matrix — I can tell you the three checks each caught a different class, and that the combination went to zero incidents.”

Zero-tolerance drift isn’t realistic — what is the false-positive cost?

Every difference is investigated, so the cost is investigator hours; the benefit is no unexplained difference between books. A non-zero tolerance would price each unit at up to $30 of exposure; the exit bar made zero crisp.

Say: “A zero tolerance costs investigation time, and I chose it because an unexplained difference between books is a customer promise I cannot see.”

What can your detector not see?

Undersell — a one-sided detector. A wrong rule that starves a channel never goes negative; the asymmetry makes that deliberate but unusual undersell needs a separate watch — which is what I would add.

Say: “The Negative AFS screen is blind to undersell by design; the missing control is a watch on unusual undersell, and I would add it.”

20-second version + NACF bridgeNEW

20-second version. At the Utah DC I owned exception control for the published AFS: a negative AFS decomposes into three cause classes — duplicate event, feed latency, override — each with an owner, every AFS-changing act has a screen, a role and an audit row, and drift tolerance is zero; negatives were clean at T+1, drift zero on consecutive days, and customer-facing oversell went to zero through both go-lives.

NACF bridge. NACF’s equivalent is an ASIN whose committed units exceed what the building can back — a double receive scan, dock-to-stock lag (the dwell side of CAP-D) or a manual adjustment. I would stand up the same map — cause class, owner, screen, role, audit row — and a zero-tolerance reconciliation between dock-received and system-available counts before an FC ramps.

U7 — The 836-row issues log as the building’s readiness instrument — severity-first reading, and the 42 rows where the dock, the floor, the power and the footprint were the problemNEW

LPs this story serves here: Dive Deep (primary — three cuts of one book: severity, flow, month) · Insist on the Highest Standards (one grammar; a visible deferral over a buried blocker) · Ownership (rows with no system owner: conveyor, aisle 45, power, guardrails) · Earn Trust (the worst number in the room was mine to say out loud)
Process family: Plan / Execute — program readiness measurement: one log, one grammar, severity-first triage, and the physical-node slice of the defect book
Honesty label. Record-from-docs, computed with pandas from UDC_Consolidated_Issues_Log_for_BY_WMS-Locus - Copy (sheet 1; 836 rows × 19 columns; an .xlsx container with a .CSV extension): status, priority, category, type, operation, functional area, owner and date counts are exact. Where I cut a free-text field (Operation; Functional Area) I say which labels I counted. Limits I will state before I am asked: the file has no close-date column, so I cannot compute time-to-close or reconstruct what was open at go/no-go from it; it is a late snapshot (last row 2025-10-01); the building slice is my own label grouping. The ACL request-row counts (Dev 310 · QA 255 · Perf 200 · Prod 221) are record from my Apple workbook (C2), not recomputed. Dollar figures in Table 4 are modeled/illustrative (30 min a row; $110/h blended, as S5/U5) and are not claimed as savings.
Leadership Principles carried — one-line proof each
Dive Deep. I read one 836-row book three ways — severity, flow, month — and the three cuts said different things: Blockers and Criticals closed at 99.4% and 97.1% against 60.0% for Lows; Dotcom was the most severe flow; June was a spike with a 0.35 monthly decay.
Insist on the Highest Standards. One log, one grammar, no side lists; a parking-lot status meant ‘decided not now’ in writing with an owner — never silently deleted — and the weekly review read Blocker and Critical rows first.
Ownership. 17 of the 42 building rows (conveyor, aisle 45, Locus power, guardrails, chargers) sat with Operations, Engineering or Maintenance — not IT; they went into the same log with a name and a date.
Earn Trust. Any VP could open the log any Monday and see what the team saw; the worst number in the room was mine to say out loud, including the 271-row June spike.
60-second business-first pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. The Sephora Utah DC conversion ran about thirty build tracks and sixty-plus stakeholders over about a dozen impacted systems; status by opinion would have drowned it. Task. Build the instrument that told us whether the building was ready — before cutover week, not during it. Action. One consolidated issues log with one grammar — status, priority, operation, functional area, owner on every row — read Blocker and Critical first at a weekly two-block review, with a parking-lot status for ‘decided not now’ and one coupled build plan beside it. Result. 836 rows; 714 closed — 85.4% — and 98.0% of the 441 Blockers and Criticals; the June go-live spike of 271 rows burned down 65, 21, 14, 4; and the 42 rows where the building itself was the problem — dock, appointments, conveyor, aisle 45, Locus power, footprint — were 92.9% closed with none of the open three a Blocker or Critical. Zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives.

Situation / Task — the business process problem, scope, scale, baselines

S — About thirty build tracks, sixty-plus stakeholders, about a dozen impacted systems and a building that kept shipping. The risk I was asked to instrument was go-live readiness: defect burn-down, track completion, cutover preparedness. Issues arrived from IT, DC Ops, Engineering, Maintenance and the robotics vendor, in different vocabularies, with no shared priority scale.

T — Make a surprise show up in a Monday review, not in cutover week: one log, one build plan, one runbook — three views of the same dataset — and a way to read the book that put the dangerous rows first.

The book as it stands (computed): 836 rows · 714 closed (85.4%) · 122 not closed, of which 112 carry an open status and 10 have a blank status · Blocker 164 + Critical 277 = 441 (52.8% of the book) · bugs are only 115 rows (13.8%) — the rest are requirements, actions, questions and parked decisions: a work-triage system, not a defect list.

Action — the process / operating-model redesign, in gated phases
  • Discovery (3/29 → 4/30) — agreed the grammar before the first real row: one status ladder (A open → F closed), one priority scale (0 Blocker → 4 Low), operation, functional area and owner on every row; one owner per row. Exit bar: grammar signed by IT and DC Ops; no side lists.
  • Plan & Design (5/15 → 8/14) — decomposed the build plan into about thirty tracks, holiday freeze and critical path visible; physical-node work (conveyor, aisle 45 relabel, guardrails, footprint defaults) entered the log with a named owner and a date rather than living in a facilities thread. Exit bar: every track mapped to an E2E gate; building rows owned.
  • Build & Test (Aug → Jan/Apr) — weekly two-block review (30 min IT, 30 min business) reading Blocker and Critical first; monthly arc watched — about 90 rows in each of February and March from the January bake plus Locus physical testing; Locus-side rows held to 86 (10.3%). Exit bar: every red row has a name and a date.
  • Cutover & Go-Live (January; May 1 → Jul 1) — the log as the readiness gate: both go-lives read burn-down curves, not confidence; network ACL request workbooks per environment as the access gate (Dev 310 · QA 255 · Perf 200 · Prod 221 rows). Exit bar: Blocker + Critical bar from the go/no-go; access gate closed per environment.
  • Hypercare / Close (Jun → Oct) — the 271-row June spike read as healing: 65 → 21 → 14 → 4; the open tail stayed visible and owned. Exit bar: decay, not plateau; every open row owned.

Influence and ownership. Mine: the grammar, the weekly read order, the parking-lot rule, the coupling of log, plan and runbook, and the readiness call off counted rows. Sephora IT/WMS (342 rows), Operations (111), Engineering/Maintenance and Locus (86) owned and closed their rows.

Designed the process; others own it now. The log, the plan and the runbook are one dataset in three views, so the next program inherits an instrument, not a habit. Trade-off put to leadership: attention — the parking-lot status made deferral explicit so the Blocker-and-Critical half of the book got the room’s time.

Results — $ · # · % · time · customer · quality
  • $ — modeled, not claimed: triaging 836 rows at 30 minutes each ≈ 418 h × $110 = $45,980 ≈ 1.45 weeks of the modeled ≈$31,731/week benefit — the log pays back if it prevents about a week and a half of slip.
  • # — 836 rows; 714 closed; 441 Blocker + Critical, 432 closed; June spike 271 → 4; building slice 42 rows, 39 closed (computed from the file).
  • % — 85.4% closed overall; 98.0% of Blocker + Critical closed; closure falls with severity — 99.4%, 97.1%, 75.4%, 69.7%, 60.0% — the read order showed in the curve.
  • Time — fifteen months discovery through go-live; June spike decay ≈0.35 per month, half-life ≈20 days.
  • Customer — zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives (record).
  • Quality — one grammar, no side lists; deferral explicit; every red row with a name and a date.

Probing — five facets, then the hardest follow-upsNEW
The five facets — where each one lives in this story
1 · Data analysis. Grain: one row per issue; 19 columns. Joins: none — the discipline was one table. Cuts: severity × status (closure gradient); operation (five flows by exact label: 675 of 836 rows, 161 free-text or blank); month; owner; functional area (dock and building labels). Revealed: closure rates fall monotonically with severity; Dotcom was the most severe flow (64.5% Blocker + Critical); the June spike was 88.2% Go-Live category and decayed fast; the building slice ran more severe than the book (64.3% vs 52.8%) but z = 1.50 — directional, not proven.
2 · Solution design. Options: (A) status by opinion in decks; (B) one tool per track; (C) one log with one grammar, coupled to one build plan and one runbook (chosen). A and B make two rows describe one issue and the counts lie; C makes the count the gate.
3 · Math & data logic. Closure = closed ÷ rows by severity; Blocker + Critical share = 441 ÷ 836 = 52.8%; decay r = (4 ÷ 271)^(1/4) = 0.349, half-life = ln 2 ÷ −ln r = 0.66 months ≈ 20 days; building-slice z = (0.643 − 0.528) ÷ √(0.528 × 0.472 ÷ 42) = 1.50.
4 · Implementation. Five gated phases; the log, the build plan (~30 tracks) and the cutover runbook (343 tasks) as three views; ACL request workbooks per environment as the access gate; weekly two-block review. Risks: duplicate rows, free-text drift, priority inflation.
5 · Probe questions. Below: is 836 a defect fire, how do you know the log is honest, what does the monthly shape tell you, what is in the building slice, and the three trap drills.
Facet 5 — probe questions (challenge Q&A tuned to this interviewer)

Infra — 836 issues. Isn’t that a defect fire?

No — the log proves it. Bugs are 115 rows, 13.8%; the rest are requirements (159), action items, questions (23) and parked decisions. It was the program’s work-triage system. What I do read as a risk signal is that 52.8% of the book is Blocker or Critical — and then the closure gradient: 99.4% of Blockers closed against 60.0% of Lows. If everything had been priority one, the curve would be flat; it is not.

Infra — what did the log say about the physical building?

Forty-two rows — 5.0% of the book — carried dock and appointment flow (23) or floor, power and footprint (19): TMS appointments with no POs attached in training (Blocker), retail pallet-build positions not available (Critical), ‘Facilities team remove half of conveyor’ (Critical), remove the west side of aisle 45 and relabel (Critical), Locus IDF temporary power until January 6 (Blocker), remove the yellow guardrail between retail and dotcom (Blocker), update large and standard footprint per SKU with large as the default (Blocker). 27 of the 42 were Blocker or Critical — 64.3% — and 39 closed; the three open were not B+C.

Infra — what does the June spike tell you?

271 rows in one month — 32.4% of the book — 239 of them Go-Live category. Then 65, 21, 14, 4: each month retained about a third of the last, r = 0.35, half-life about 20 days. A spike with fast decay is a healthy cutover; one that plateaus is a design problem. I would have wanted that picture on the wall from the first hundred rows.

Infra — how do you know the log is honest?

One row per issue, one grammar, a weekly read where the worst number was mine to say, a dedupe step in the rhythm — two rows for one issue and the counts lie — and ‘parking lot’ meaning decided-not-now in writing. The file does not carry close dates, so I will not claim a time-to-close from it.

Infra — why were Locus rows only a tenth of the book?

86 rows — 10.3% — because the robotics workstream had its own rhythm and owner, and the 98% gate with the throttle caught the zone-logic problem in week two instead of the floor. They were severe — 65 of 86 Blocker or Critical, 75.6% — and every one closed.

Spoken statement

“I treated the program’s issues log as an instrument and read it three ways. By severity: of 836 rows, 441 were Blocker or Critical and 98% of those closed, with closure falling to 60% for Lows — so the weekly read was working on the dangerous end. By flow: Dotcom was the most severe. By month: the June go-live spike of 271 rows decayed 65, 21, 14, 4 — about a third a month — which is what a healing cutover looks like. And the part an infrastructure leader cares about: 42 rows were the building itself — dock and appointments, conveyor, aisle 45, Locus power, footprint — 64% Blocker or Critical, 39 closed, none of the three open ones severe. The statistics are directional — z of 1.5 — and the file has no close dates, so I will not claim a time-to-close. The point is that the building’s problems were rows with names and dates, not a hallway conversation.”

If challenged

“Priority inflation — 52.8% Blocker or Critical isn’t a priority scale.”

It’s a fair flag and I’d rather say it first. Two answers: the closure gradient — 99.4%, 97.1%, 75.4%, 69.7%, 60.0% — shows the scale differentiated in practice; and the book was a go-live book, 331 rows Go-Live category, where severity runs high by construction. What I would add is a monthly check on the Blocker + Critical share so inflation shows as a trend.

“Your building slice is a label grouping you chose.”

Yes — so I ran three definitions: narrow 31 rows (64.5%, z 1.31), base 42 (64.3%, z 1.50), broad 104 (66.3%, z 2.78). The direction is the same in all three; only the broad one clears 2σ, and it is the loosest definition, so I call the result directional.

“Prove the Blocker + Critical bar was met at go/no-go from this file.”

I can’t — the file is a late snapshot with no close dates; it shows 9 B+C still open on 2025-10-01 (1 Blocker, 8 Critical), which postdates both go-lives. The go/no-go read came from the weekly review state at the time.

Behavioral pack — four separate 90-second STAR answers, each from a different moment of the storyNEW
Dive Deep · AIQB Q4Q1. “Walk me through a big problem or issue in your organization that you helped to solve.”

The problem was that a program of about thirty build tracks was going to run on opinion, and the first fix was to count. I made one consolidated log with five fields on every row and read it three ways. By severity, 441 of 836 rows were Blocker or Critical and closure fell with severity — 99.4% of Blockers, 60% of Lows. By flow, Dotcom ran most severe. By month, the shape told me the June spike was healing: 271, then 65, 21, 14, 4. What was missing was the monthly shape itself — it was a manual read well into the program — and I filled the gap by building the arc view once I saw it, which I would now have on the wall from the first hundred rows. The result was that both go-lives read readiness from rows, with zero customer-facing incidents.

Number to land: 836 rows · 441 B+C · 271 → 4 · r ≈ 0.35  ·  Pivot to NACF: NACF launch readiness is the same instrument — doors tested, appointment slots scheduled, UPH validated — as counted rows read severity-first.  ·  Do NOT say: that 836 issues means 836 defects — bugs are 115.
Insist on the Highest Standards · AIQB Q6Q2. “Describe the most significant continuous improvement project that you led.”

The catalyst was that two rows describing one issue make every count lie, so the improvement was a grammar. One log, one status ladder, one priority scale, an owner on every row, and a parking-lot status that meant ‘decided not now’ in writing with an owner — never silently deleted. Dedupe was part of the weekly rhythm. The result is a book where any VP could open the file on a Monday and see what the team saw: 714 of 836 closed, 98% of the Blockers and Criticals, and the open tail of 122 visible and owned. I would rather defend a visible deferral than bury a blocker, and a log that calls everything priority one is where blockers hide.

Number to land: 85.4% closed · 98.0% of B+C · 122 open, owned  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF I would run the same grammar on a launch register: one row per risk, an owner, a date, a severity.  ·  Do NOT say: that the file proves time-to-close; it has no close dates.
Ownership · AIQB Q3Q3. “Give me an example of an initiative you undertook because you saw that it could benefit the whole company or your customers, but wasn’t within any group’s individual responsibility so nothing was being done.”

The building itself did not belong to the systems program, so I made it belong to the log. Forty-two rows were dock, floor, power or footprint: remove half of a conveyor, remove the west side of aisle 45 and relabel, get Locus IDF temporary power until January 6, remove the guardrail between retail and dotcom, set the large footprint as the SKU default. 17 of those 42 sat with Operations, Engineering or Maintenance, not IT. Putting them in the same log with a name and a date meant a Blocker about power had the same Monday review as a Blocker about a WMS interface. 39 of the 42 closed, and the three still open were not Blocker or Critical. The conveyor and the power were not mine; making them visible and dated was.

Number to land: 42 rows · 17 non-IT owners · 39 closed · 0 open B+C  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF the dock, the floor and the power are launch dependencies too — they go on the same register as the systems.  ·  Do NOT say: that I fixed the conveyor or the power; Operations and Maintenance did.
Earn Trust · AIQB Q6Q4. “Tell me about a time when you uncovered a significant problem in your team.”

The June spike was the biggest number the program ever produced, and I said it first. 271 rows in one month — a third of the book — 239 of them Go-Live category. I put it in the Monday review the week it appeared with the shape that explained it: the January bake and Locus physical testing had given a ramp of about ninety a month, the combined go-live surfaced everything at once, and the decay afterwards — 65, 21, 14, 4 — was what healing looks like. A spike that decays fast is a healthy cutover; one that plateaus is a design problem. Because the number was in the room before anyone asked, nobody escalated a surprise, and the readiness call was made on rows.

Number to land: 271 rows · 88.2% Go-Live · decay ≈ 0.35/month  ·  Pivot to NACF: At NACF the worst number in the WBR is the one to say first, with the curve that explains it.  ·  Do NOT say: that the spike was a defect fire; it was the combined go-live surfacing.
Same story, three angles — data · conflict/influence/backbone · failure-learningNEW
Data angle · Dive Deep“836 rows, and the closure rate falls with severity: 99.4%, 97.1%, 75.4%, 69.7%, 60.0%.”
Headline metric: 98.0% of 441 B+C closed; r = 0.35
Three cuts of one book — severity, flow, month. Honest limit: no close-date column, so no time-to-close; the Operation field is free text (161 rows outside five flows).		Conflict / influence angle · Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit“Well-meaning teams wanted every improvement in the release; a parking-lot status said ‘decided not now’ in writing.”
Headline metric: 8 Parking Lot rows; Blocker + Critical first
I defended visible deferral over a buried blocker; the cost was explicit — the room’s attention went to the dangerous half of the book. The disagreement was resolved by writing the deferral, with an owner, not by winning an argument.		Failure / learning angle · Insist on the Highest Standards“The arc — ramp, spike, decay — was a manual read well into the program.”
Headline metric: shape view from month 1, not month ~6
Record do-differently: I would build the monthly shape view from the first hundred rows; the counts were always there, the picture was not. Second: 52.8% Blocker + Critical is a priority-inflation risk I should have trended monthly.
Trap drills — the three nastiest follow-ups, with numeric answersNEW
“How do you know the log was the reason go-live went well, not the team?”

I don’t, and I won’t claim it. What the log gives is a testable statement: 98.0% of Blockers and Criticals closed and the June spike decayed at r ≈ 0.35. A team without the log could have done the same; what the log changed is that the readiness call was made on counted rows, and a surprise surfaced on a Monday.

Say: “I can’t separate the log from the team; I can show the readiness call came off counted rows and the spike decayed in line with a healthy cutover.”

“Your building-slice finding — is it significant?”

Directional only: 27 of 42 = 64.3% vs 52.8%, z = 1.50, about p = 0.13. Across three definitions the direction holds; only the broad one clears 2σ. The decision it informs is cheap — put physical rows in the same log with owners — and holds regardless.

Say: “z of 1.5 — directional, not proven; I would act on it because the action costs almost nothing.”

“What number would have made you stop the go-live?”

One open Blocker at the go/no-go read, or a June-style spike that plateaued instead of decaying — a decay ratio above about 0.8 for two months. The first is the record bar; the 0.8 threshold is my own illustrative plateau test.

Say: “A Blocker open at go/no-go, or a spike that stops decaying — I would call that a design problem.”

20-second version + NACF bridgeNEW

20-second version. I ran Sephora’s UDC program on one 836-row issues log with one grammar, read Blocker and Critical first: 85.4% closed, 98% of the Blockers and Criticals, the 271-row June spike decaying 65, 21, 14, 4 — and 42 rows where the dock, the floor, the power and the footprint were the problem, every one with a name and a date.

NACF bridge. NACF’s launch register is the same instrument. The dock rows are CAP-A in miniature — appointments created, placards printing, pallet-build positions available before the first trailer — and the footprint rows are the CAP-C input: a SKU with no footprint or CubiScan record defaults a slot size, and a wrong default is wasted cube at the 85% effective-capacity line. I would read the register severity-first, with the dock row at the top.

Backup bench — use only if a primary doesn't fit

Four fallbacks for an infrastructure owner: S13 (Manhattan — Cyber Monday at 4 AM; the incident-response / Bias for Action alternate to S18, and the older pair for S2's throttle), S5 (Sephora — the architecture council without owning a system; governance of an about-a-dozen-systems change, same program family as S2 — never pair S5 with S2 for this interviewer unless asked for a second UDC example) and S15 (Sephora — the QA cadence conversion; the people / Hire & Develop question — Josyula's primary, so only if the question demands it); plus a doc-derived fourth from the Flexport program record — the Shipment-Visibility pilot roll-out and its four testing gates (launch discipline behind published graduation criteria; record-from-docs labels; same program family as Tripathy's S11, facts kept identical to her packet). Tell S2 and S18 first.

Bench discipline. The two (or four) primaries above are the stories to tell first. Reach for a bench story only when the question genuinely does not fit a primary, or when the interviewer asks for "another example" on an LP already spent. Each entry is a 60-second pitch, a compressed STAR, one results line and two probes — enough to answer and stop; no full probing bank or derivation. Where a bench story is another interviewer's primary, the red note says so: use it only if the question demands it, and keep the facts identical to that packet.

Backup — only if askedS13 — Manhattan — Cyber Monday, 4 AM: allocation across 3 DCs and 100 stores when the scheduler died

LPs this backup can serve: Bias for Action (primary) · Customer Obsession (primary backup — the promise ranked first) · Deliver Results
Basis. OMS consultant on allocation, promising and the OMS–WMS handshake; engineering fixed the scheduler once I isolated it; the post-mortem mechanisms were mine to drive. $8M / $6M / 99% are record; the margin figures are my model at benchmark rates. Older story — if asked for recent, pair with the UDC robotics throttle (S2): same muscle, 2024–25.
60-second pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Manhattan OMS consultant on allocation and promising across 3 DCs and 100 stores; 4 AM Cyber Monday the VP called — backorders piling with inventory present, ~$6M of sales at risk against a 95% fill-rate goal. Task. Own the response before the root cause was known. Action. Discovery: the contradiction (ATP > 0, backorders rising) was the entry bar to declare an incident. Plan & Design: I ranked by promise-at-risk, not FIFO; node choice = the promise — near 0.85 days vs far 4.60. Build & Test: one bridge, every rebalance timestamped; reason-coded alerts I had already built. Cutover: rebalanced so no DC drowned; status every two hours until 9 PM. Hypercare: root cause — the allocation scheduler died silently at the daylight-saving change; the post-mortem shipped a heartbeat, a DST checklist, a 3σ page rule and a rehearsed rebalance playbook. Lesson: decide reversibly, log it, find the cause later. Result. Primary metric: peak fill rate — 95% goal → 99% delivered. ~$8M of peak sales protected against ~$6M at risk (record); my model puts that at ≈$3.2M of gross margin at a 40% benchmark margin; structural stockouts −20% over the engagement.

Compressed STAR
  • S — 4 AM Cyber Monday: backorder lines piling with inventory present; promises aging; ~$6M at risk against a 95% fill goal; nobody had written a clearing rule for failure.
  • T — Own the response before the cause was known, without making it worse.
  • A — Declared the incident on the contradiction; clearing rule — promise-at-risk, not FIFO — written on the bridge log in the first hour so nobody re-argued it at noon; rebalanced across three DCs; two-hourly status; isolated the DST scheduler death through my own reason-coded alerts; blameless post-mortem → heartbeat, DST checklist, 3σ page rule, rehearsed playbook.
  • R — Fill 99% vs the 95% goal; ~$8M protected vs ~$6M at risk (record pair); structural stockouts −20% over the engagement; the next peak ran boring.
Results line

Peak fill 99% vs the 95% goal (record) · ~$8M of peak sales protected vs ~$6M at risk (record pair — one-time sales that didn't die, never a saving) · ≈$3.2M gross margin at a 40% benchmark (modeled) · structural stockouts −20% over the engagement (record) · detection moved from a VP phone call to one heartbeat interval.

Two probes

Why act before you knew the cause?

Because waiting had an hourly price — the queue was aging while inventory sat idle — and a reversible, logged intervention did not. I checked with engineering first that manual clears could not corrupt allocation, timestamped every rebalance, and hunted the cause in parallel with alerts I had already built.

Isn't this a hero story?

The event is; the post-mortem is the point. Carrying the first stretch solo is one of my do-differentlys. What I would present as the result is the mechanism set — heartbeat, DST checklist, 3σ page rule, rehearsed playbook — and that the next peak ran boring.

Canon numbers (basis labels): 99% fill vs 95% goal (record) · ~$8M protected vs ~$6M at risk (record) · 3 DCs / 100 stores (record) · near 0.85 d vs far 4.60 d (record) · −20% structural stockouts (record) · ≈$3.2M margin at 40% (modeled). Never "$2MM baseline"; never present $8M as a saving.

Two probes

How do you get sixty people to sign LOE you don't control?

A template, a deadline and a private conversation each — so nobody learned their cost in the room. The council was then asked for three separable decisions it could take one at a time; separability was the design.

What did you refuse, and did it hold?

Retuning the reserve math and folding in the MCS decommission — both written, signed and held; later scope requests were answered from the pack in writing. The legitimate concern (a second live warehouse becoming a second truth) was converted into the design as a monitored shadow copy, because converting a concern costs less than winning it.

Canon numbers (basis labels): ~80 participants / ~15 functions; about a dozen impacted systems (record) · 5/15 → 7/31 → 8/14; LOE 7/2 (record dates) · escalations 0; fence breaches 0 (record) · ≈$0.5M one-time (modeled). Never a dollar as record; never pair with S1 or S2 for the same interviewer.

Backup — only if askedDoc bench — Flexport — the Shipment-Visibility pilot roll-out: graduated waves behind six published criteria, four testing gates, monthly GO/NO-GO (launch discipline)

LPs this backup can serve: Insist on the Highest Standards (primary — gates that fire) · Deliver Results · Bias for Action (monthly GO/NO-GO cadence) · Customer Obsession (CSAT inside the gate)
Basis. Doc-derived micro-story from the program record (the SV pilot roll-out schedule and the SV product testing approach, 2021–22) — every figure is record-from-docs. Same program family as Tripathy's S11: if she has already taken S11 deep, use this only for a launch-discipline question and keep the canon numbers identical to her packet. Program-lead voice: I owned the rollout design and the gates; data, ops and CS ran calibration, verification and UAT. Not in the master packet's S-numbering; supplements canon, never overrides it.
60-second pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. A visibility product with public commitments was running a free pilot — every boarded client cost engineering, data and ops capacity before a dollar of revenue, and boarding clients before the data was trustworthy would manufacture churn at scale. Task. Design a roll-out that could say no — and make "no" a rule, not a judgment call. Action. Graduated waves: wave 1 two named clients (Ta Chen International, FAM Brands), wave 2 four of a seven-client pool, wave 3 twenty-plus, full roll-out H2 2022 — each client entering on a ~50-container calibration and graduating only on six published criteria: verification SLA 90% reviewed within 24h, data quality ≥90% and at least the incumbent Crux feed, milestone estimation ≥80%, performance to standards, ≥100 containers with key features live, CSAT ≥ the consignee average. Four testing gates stood before any launch: a 100-container live test watched end-to-end, biweekly bug bashes on the client job stories, shadow accounts with real Crux data in dedicated demo accounts, and UAT gated on data quality — 80% of shipments minor-to-no issues — not on the calendar. GO/NO-GO decisions ran monthly. Result. A roll-out that never had to roll back: the graduation rule, not heroics, set the pace — the launch-discipline version of S11's canon (waves held on the criteria; the held wave became the cleanest one). record-from-docs

Compressed STAR
  • S — A free pilot under public commitments, with an acquired product (Crux) whose customers would transition mid-roll-out — sequencing and quality bars were the whole game.
  • T — Own the roll-out design: wave structure, graduation criteria, testing gates and the GO/NO-GO cadence.
  • A — Published six graduation criteria before wave 1; a ~50-container calibration per client as the entry bar; four testing gates including shadow accounts with real Crux data and a UAT data-quality bar (80% minor-to-no-issues) before any client UAT started; monthly GO/NO-GO from November; wave sizes 2 → 4-of-7 → 20+ → full H2 2022.
  • R — A roll-out with zero rollbacks (S11 canon); every gate that fired was a rule firing, not a favor refused — the held wave became the cleanest wave.
Results line

Waves 2 → 4-of-7 → 20+ → full H2 2022 (record-from-docs; matches S11 canon) · six graduation criteria published before wave 1 (canon) · ~50-container calibration entry bar (canon) · four testing gates with UAT gated at 80% minor-to-no-issues (record-from-docs) · monthly GO/NO-GO cadence (record-from-docs) · zero rollbacks (canon).

Two probes

Why gate UAT on data quality instead of a date?

Because a client UAT against dirty data manufactures false confidence — the client signs off on a product the data then betrays. The 80% minor-to-no-issues bar meant UAT started when the product could survive a skeptical user's first week; the date followed the data, never the reverse. Shadow accounts with real Crux data let us find the defects before the client did.

What did the calibration actually buy?

A verified baseline per client before scale: ~50 containers through verification with the SLA clock running, so the data-quality and estimation criteria were measured on the client's own freight, not on our best lanes. It converted "ready" from a feeling into a measurement — and it made the wave-2 selection from the seven-client pool defensible to the clients not picked.

Canon numbers (basis labels): waves 2 → 4-of-7 → 20+ → full H2 2022 (record-from-docs, matches S11 canon) · six criteria incl. DQ ≥90% · ETA ≥80% · SLA 90% within 24h · ≥100 containers + features · stability · CSAT ≥ consignee average (canon; the pilot schedule phrases the SLA as 24–48h — canon wins, footnoted in Tripathy's packet) · ~50-container calibration (canon) · 100-container test · bug bashes · shadow accounts · UAT 80% bar (record-from-docs). Never: a client-revenue claim (the pilot was free); never merge with Crux-transition ARR.
